**Thessaloniki** ([/ˌθɛsələˈniːki/](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:IPA/English); [Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_language): Θεσσαλονίκη [\[θesaloˈnici\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:IPA/Greek) [//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/5/5c/Thessaloniki.ogg/Thessaloniki.ogg.mp3](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/5/5c/Thessaloniki.ogg/Thessaloniki.ogg.mp3)<sup>ⓘ</sup>; [also known by various spellings and names](#Names_and_etymology)) is the capital and largest city of the [administrative region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Administrative_regions_of_Greece) of [Central Macedonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Macedonia), and the second-largest city in [Greece](12108.md). It is the largest city of [Northern Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Greece), with over 300,000 in the city proper and slightly over one million inhabitants in its [metropolitan area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thessaloniki_metropolitan_area), the [capital](181337.md) of the [geographic region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geographic_regions_of_Greece) of [Macedonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Macedonia_\(Greece\)), and the [Decentralized Administration of Macedonia and Thrace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Decentralized_Administration_of_Macedonia_and_Thrace).[^cite_note-7][^cite_note-8] It is also known in Greek as *i Symprotévousa* (η Συμπρωτεύουσα), literally "the co-capital",[^cite_note-Greek_Experience-9] a reference to its historical status as the "co-reigning" city (*Symvasilévousa*/Συμβασιλεύουσα) of the [Byzantine Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_Empire) alongside [Constantinople](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constantinople).[^cite_note-Neoclassical-10]

Thessaloniki is located on the [Thermaic Gulf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thermaic_Gulf), at the northwest corner of the [Aegean Sea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aegean_Sea). It is bounded on the west by the [delta of the Axios](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Axios_Delta_National_Park). The [municipality of Thessaloniki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thessaloniki_\(municipality\)), the historical centre, had a population of 319,045 in 2021,[^cite_note-2021census-3] while the [Thessaloniki metropolitan area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thessaloniki_metropolitan_area) had 1,006,112 inhabitants and the [greater region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thessaloniki_\(regional_unit\)) had 1,092,919.[^cite_note-auto1-5][^cite_note-urban_metro-4] It is Greece's second major economic, industrial, commercial and political centre, and a major transportation hub for Greece and southeastern Europe, notably through the [Port of Thessaloniki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Thessaloniki).[^cite_note-Development-11] The city is renowned for its festivals, events and vibrant cultural life in general.[^cite_note-Lonely_Planet_Thessaloniki-12] Events such as the [Thessaloniki International Fair](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thessaloniki_International_Fair) and the [Thessaloniki International Film Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thessaloniki_International_Film_Festival) are held annually. Thessaloniki was the 2014 [European Youth Capital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Youth_Capital). The city's main university, [Aristotle University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aristotle_University), is the largest in Greece and the [Balkans](4829.md).[^cite_note-AUTH-13]

The city was founded in 315 BC by [Cassander](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cassander) of Macedon, who named it after his wife [Thessalonike](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thessalonike_of_Macedon), daughter of [Philip II of Macedon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philip_II_of_Macedon) and sister of [Alexander the Great](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_the_Great). It was built 40 km southeast of [Pella](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pella), the capital of the [Kingdom of Macedonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Macedonia). An important metropolis by the Roman period, Thessaloniki was the second largest and wealthiest city of the [Byzantine Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_Empire). It [was conquered by the Ottomans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Thessalonica_\(1422%E2%80%931430\)) in 1430 and remained an important seaport and multi-ethnic metropolis during the nearly five centuries of [Turkish rule](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_Greece), with churches, mosques, and synagogues co-existing side by side. From the 16th to the 20th centuries, it was the only [Jewish-majority city in Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Jews_in_Thessaloniki). It passed from the [Ottoman Empire](22278.md) to the [Kingdom of Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Greece) on 8 November 1912. Thessaloniki exhibits [Byzantine architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_architecture), including [numerous Paleochristian and Byzantine monuments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paleochristian_and_Byzantine_monuments_of_Thessaloniki), a [World Heritage Site](44940.md), and several [Roman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Empire), Ottoman and [Sephardic Jewish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sephardic_Jewish) structures.

In 2013, *[National Geographic Magazine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Geographic_\(magazine\))* included Thessaloniki in its top tourist destinations worldwide,[^cite_note-best_trips_2013-14] while in 2014 *[Financial Times](136566.md)* FDI magazine (Foreign Direct Investments) declared Thessaloniki as the best mid-sized European city of the future for human capital and lifestyle.[^cite_note-Η_Θεσσαλονίκη_αναδείχθηκε_σε_ευρωπαϊκή_πόλη_του_μέλλοντος_για_το_2014_Best_Mid-_Sized_city_for_human_capital_&_lifestyle-15][^cite_note-European_Cities_and_Regions_of_the_Future_2014/15-16]

## Names and etymology

See also: [Names of Thessaloniki in different languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Names_of_Thessaloniki_in_different_languages) and [Thessalonike of Macedon § Legend of Thessalonike](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thessalonike_of_Macedon#Legend_of_Thessalonike)



Inscription reading "To Queen [Thessalonike](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thessalonike_of_Macedon), (Daughter) of [Philip](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philip_II_of_Macedon)", [Archaeological Museum of Thessaloniki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Archaeological_Museum_of_Thessaloniki)

The original name of the city was Θεσσαλονίκη *Thessaloníkē*. It was named after the princess [Thessalonike of Macedon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thessalonike_of_Macedon), the half-sister of [Alexander the Great](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_the_Great), whose name means "Thessalian victory", from [Θεσσαλός](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/%CE%98%CE%B5%CF%83%CF%83%CE%B1%CE%BB%CF%8C%CF%82) Thessalos, and [Νίκη](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/%CE%9D%CE%AF%CE%BA%CE%B7) 'victory' ([Nike](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nike_\(mythology\))), honoring the Macedonian victory at the [Battle of Crocus Field](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Crocus_Field) (353/352 BC). A popular Greek legend has it that Thessalonike became a [mermaid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mermaid) who lived in the [Aegean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aegean_Sea) after the [death of Alexander the Great](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Death_of_Alexander_the_Great).[^cite_note-17][^cite_note-18]

Minor variants are also found, including Θετταλονίκη *Thettaloníkē*,[^cite_note-19][^cite_note-20] Θεσσαλονίκεια *Thessaloníkeia*,[^cite_note-21] Θεσσαλονείκη *Thessaloneíkē*, and Θεσσαλονικέων *Thessalonikéon*.[^cite_note-22][^cite_note-23]

The name Σαλονίκη *Saloníki* is first attested in Greek in the [Chronicle of the Morea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chronicle_of_the_Morea) (14th century), and is common in [folk songs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Folk_song), but it must have originated earlier, as [al-Idrisi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad_al-Idrisi) called it *Salunik* already in the 12th century. It is the basis for the city's name in other languages: Солѹнъ (*Solunŭ*) in [Old Church Slavonic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Church_Slavonic), סאלוניקו[^cite_note-24][^cite_note-\:0-25] (*Saloniko*) in [Judeo-Spanish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judaeo-Spanish) (שאלוניקי prior to the 19th century[^cite_note-\:0-25]) סלוניקי (*Saloniki*) in [Hebrew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebrew_language), *Selanik* in [Albanian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albanian_language), سلانیك (*Selânik*) in [Ottoman Turkish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_Turkish_language) and *Selanik* in [modern Turkish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_language), *Salonicco* in [Italian](14708.md), *Solun* or Солун in the [local](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slavic_speakers_of_Greek_Macedonia) and neighboring [South Slavic languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Slavic_languages), Салоники (*Saloníki*) in [Russian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_language), *Sãrunã* in [Aromanian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aromanian_language)[^cite_note-26] and *Săruna* in [Megleno-Romanian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Megleno-Romanian_language).[^cite_note-27]

In English, the city can be called Thessaloniki, Salonika, Thessalonica, Salonica, Thessalonika, Saloniki, Thessalonike, or Thessalonice. In printed texts, the most common name and spelling until the early 20th century was Thessalonica ([/ˌθɛsələˈnaɪkə, ˌθɛsəˈlɒnɪkə/](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:IPA/English)), matching the Latin name; through most of rest of the 20th century, it was Salonika ([/səˈlɒnɪkə, ˌsæləˈniːkə/](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:IPA/English)). By about 1985, the most common single name became Thessaloniki.[^cite_note-28][^cite_note-29] The forms with the Latin ending *-a* taken together remain more common than those with the phonetic Greek ending *-i* and much more common than the ancient transliteration *-e*.[^cite_note-30]

Thessaloniki was revived as the city's official name in 1912, when it joined the [Kingdom of Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Greece_\(Gl%C3%BCcksburg\)) during the [Balkan Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balkan_Wars).[^cite_note-31] In local speech, the city's name is typically pronounced with a dark and deep *[L](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Velarized_alveolar_lateral_approximant)*, characteristic of the accent of the modern [Macedonian dialect](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Varieties_of_Modern_Greek#Modern_varieties) of [Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_language).[^cite_note-32][^cite_note-33] The name is often abbreviated as Θεσ/νίκη.[^cite_note-34]

## History

Main article: [History of Thessaloniki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Thessaloniki)

### From classical antiquity to the Roman Empire



Ancient coin depicting [Cassander](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cassander), son of [Antipater](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antipater), and founder of the city of Thessaloniki

The city was founded around 315 BC by the [King Cassander of Macedon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cassander), on or near the site of the ancient town of [Therma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Therma) and 26 other local villages.[^cite_note-35][^cite_note-36] He named it after his wife [Thessalonike](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thessalonike_of_Macedon),[^cite_note-Paul_of_Tarsus-37] a half-sister of [Alexander the Great](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_the_Great) and princess of Macedonia as daughter of [Philip II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philip_II_of_Macedon). Under the kingdom of Macedonia, the city retained its own autonomy and parliament[^cite_note-Thessalonica_parliament-38] and evolved to become the most important city in Macedonia.[^cite_note-Paul_of_Tarsus-37] Nonetheless, [Pella](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pella) remained the capital of [Macedon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Macedon) until its fall.

In 128 BC, twenty years after the fall of the [Kingdom of Macedonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Macedonia_\(ancient_kingdom\)), Thessalonica was made the capital of the [Roman province of Macedonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Macedonia_\(Roman_province\)).[^cite_note-39] Thessalonica became a [free city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_city_\(antiquity\)) of the [Roman Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Republic) under [Mark Antony](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mark_Antony) in 41 BC.[^cite_note-Paul_of_Tarsus-37][^cite_note-Sacra_Pagina-40] It grew to be an important trade hub located on the *[Via Egnatia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Via_Egnatia)*,[^cite_note-LPTH-41] the road connecting [Dyrrhachium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dyrrhachium) with [Byzantium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantium),[^cite_note-Via_Egnatia-42] which facilitated trade between Thessaloniki and great centres of commerce such as [Rome](25458.md) and Byzantium.[^cite_note-Urban_history-43] Thessaloniki also lies at the southern end of the main north–south route through the Balkans along the valleys of the [Morava](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Morava) and [Axios](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vardar) river valleys, thereby linking the Balkans with the rest of Greece.[^cite_note-44] The city became the capital of one of the four Roman districts of Macedonia.[^cite_note-LPTH-41]

At the time of the Roman Empire, about 50 AD, Thessaloniki was also one of the [early centres of Christianity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_centres_of_Christianity); while on his [second missionary journey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_missionary_journey), [Paul the Apostle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_the_Apostle) visited this city's chief synagogue on three Sabbaths and sowed the seeds for Thessaloniki's first Christian church. Later, Paul wrote letters to the new church at Thessaloniki, with two letters to the church under his name appearing in the [Biblical canon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biblical_canon) as [First](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Epistle_to_the_Thessalonians) and [Second Thessalonians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Epistle_to_the_Thessalonians). Some scholars hold that the First Epistle to the Thessalonians is the first written book of the [New Testament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Testament).[^cite_note-Jewish_Annotated-45]



The fourth-century AD [Rotunda of Galerius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arch_of_Galerius_and_Rotunda), one of several Roman monuments in the city and a [UNESCO](21786641.md) [World Heritage Site](44940.md)

In 306 AD, Thessaloniki acquired a patron saint, [St. Demetrius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demetrius_of_Thessaloniki), a Christian whom Galerius is said to have put to death. Most scholars agree with [Hippolyte Delehaye](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hippolyte_Delehaye)'s theory that Demetrius was not a Thessaloniki native, but his veneration was transferred to Thessaloniki when it replaced [Sirmium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sirmium) as the main military base in the Balkans.[^cite_note-46] A [basilical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basilica) church dedicated to St. Demetrius, [Hagios Demetrios](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hagios_Demetrios), was first built in the fifth century AD and is now a [UNESCO World Heritage Site](44940.md).

When the Roman Empire was divided into the [tetrarchy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tetrarchy), Thessaloniki became the administrative capital of one of the four portions of the Empire under [Galerius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galerius) Maximianus Caesar,[^cite_note-Roman_Empire-47][^cite_note-Roman_Empire_2-48] where Galerius commissioned an imperial palace, a new [hippodrome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hippodrome), a [triumphal arch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Triumphal_arch) and a [mausoleum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mausoleum), among other structures.[^cite_note-Roman_Empire_2-48][^cite_note-Roman_Art-49][^cite_note-Canepa-50]

Thessaloniki remained the administrative center of the [Diocese of Macedonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diocese_of_Macedonia). In 379, when the Roman [Prefecture of Illyricum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Praetorian_prefecture_of_Illyricum) was divided between the East and West Roman Empires, Thessaloniki became the capital of the new Prefecture of Illyricum.[^cite_note-LPTH-41] The following year, the [Edict of Thessalonica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edict_of_Thessalonica) made [Christianity](5211.md) the state religion of the [Roman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Empire).[^cite_note-Ehler65-51] In 390, troops under the Roman Emperor [Theodosius I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theodosius_I) led a [massacre against the inhabitants of Thessalonica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Massacre_of_Thessalonica), who had risen in revolt against the detention of a favorite charioteer. By the time of the [Fall of Rome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fall_of_the_Western_Roman_Empire) in 476, Thessaloniki was the second-largest city of the [Eastern Roman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_Empire).[^cite_note-Urban_history-43]

### Byzantine era and Middle Ages

See also: [Thessalonica (theme)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thessalonica_\(theme\)), [Demetrius of Thessaloniki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demetrius_of_Thessaloniki), [Kingdom of Thessalonica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Thessalonica), [Empire of Thessalonica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Empire_of_Thessalonica), and [Zealots of Thessalonica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zealots_of_Thessalonica)



Section of the [Walls of Thessaloniki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walls_of_Thessaloniki)

From the first years of the [Byzantine Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_Empire), Thessaloniki was considered the second city in the Empire after [Constantinople](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constantinople),[^cite_note-DCCXVI-52][^cite_note-Browing-53][^cite_note-Last_Centuries-54] both in terms of wealth and size,[^cite_note-DCCXVI-52] with a population of 150,000 in the mid-12th century.[^cite_note-google-55] The city held this status until its transfer to Venetian control in 1423. In the 14th century, the city's population exceeded 100,000 to 150,000,[^cite_note-google2-56][^cite_note-Cambridge-57][^cite_note-The_Balkans-58] making it larger than [London](17867.md) at the time.[^cite_note-Progress-59]

During the sixth and seventh centuries, the area around Thessaloniki was invaded by [Avars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pannonian_Avars) and Slavs, who unsuccessfully laid siege to the city several times, as narrated in the *[Miracles of Saint Demetrius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miracles_of_Saint_Demetrius)*.[^cite_note-60] The written sources stipulate that many Slavs settled in the hinterland of Thessaloniki which became known as 'Macedonian Sclavinia'.[^cite_note-google61-61][^cite_note-62] In the ninth century, the Byzantine missionaries [Cyril and Methodius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cyril_and_Methodius), both natives of the city, created the first [literary language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Literary_language) of the Slavs, the [Old Church Slavonic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Church_Slavonic), most likely based on the Slavic dialect used in the hinterland of their hometown.[^cite_note-63][^cite_note-64][^cite_note-65][^cite_note-66][^cite_note-67]

A naval attack led by Byzantine converts to Islam (including [Leo of Tripoli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leo_of_Tripoli)) in 904 resulted in the [sack of the city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sack_of_Thessalonica_\(904\)).[^cite_note-68]



[Church of the Acheiropoietos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_of_the_Acheiropoietos) (5th century) at the city's centre

The economic expansion of the city continued through the 12th century as the rule of the [Komnenoi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Komnenoi) emperors expanded Byzantine control to the north. The city was [sacked again in 1185](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sack_of_Thessalonica_\(1185\)) by [Normans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Normans) from the [Kingdom of Sicily](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Sicily).

Thessaloniki passed out of Byzantine hands in 1204,[^cite_note-Byzantium_and_the_Crusades-69] when Constantinople was [captured](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Constantinople_\(1204\)) by the forces of the [Fourth Crusade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fourth_Crusade) and incorporated the city and its surrounding territories in the [Kingdom of Thessalonica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Thessalonica)[^cite_note-A_History-70] — which then became the largest [vassal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vassal) of the [Latin Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin_Empire). In 1224, the Kingdom of Thessalonica was overrun by the [Despotate of Epirus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Despotate_of_Epirus), a remnant of the former Byzantine Empire, under [Theodore Komnenos Doukas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theodore_Komnenos_Doukas) who crowned himself Emperor,[^cite_note-Coinage-71] and the city became the capital of the short-lived [Empire of Thessalonica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Empire_of_Thessalonica).[^cite_note-Coinage-71][^cite_note-Theodore-72][^cite_note-Late_Byzantine-73][^cite_note-History_of_Greece-74] Following his defeat at [Klokotnitsa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Klokotnitsa) however in 1230,[^cite_note-Coinage-71][^cite_note-Late_Byzantine2-75] the Empire of Thessalonica became a vassal state of the [Second Bulgarian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Bulgarian_Empire) until it was recovered again in 1246, this time by the [Nicaean Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicaean_Empire).[^cite_note-Coinage-71]

In 1342,[^cite_note-Zealots_2-76] the city saw the rise of the [Commune of the Zealots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zealots_of_Thessalonica), an anti-aristocratic party formed of sailors and the poor,[^cite_note-Zealots-77] which is nowadays described as social-revolutionary.[^cite_note-Zealots_2-76] The city was practically independent of the rest of the Empire,[^cite_note-Zealots_2-76][^cite_note-Zealots-77][^cite_note-Byzantine_State_and_Society-78] as it had its own government, a form of republic.[^cite_note-Zealots_2-76] The zealot movement was overthrown in 1350, and the city was reunited with the rest of the Empire.[^cite_note-Zealots_2-76]

The [capture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fall_of_Gallipoli) of [Gallipoli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gelibolu) by the [Ottomans](22278.md) in 1354 kicked off a rapid Turkish expansion in the southern [Balkans](4829.md), conducted both by the Ottomans themselves and by semi-independent Turkish [ghazi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghazi_\(warrior\)) warrior-bands. By 1369, the Ottomans [conquered Adrianople](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_conquest_of_Adrianople) (modern [Edirne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edirne)), which became their new capital until [1453](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fall_of_Constantinople).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFine1994377–378,_406-79] Thessalonica, ruled by [Manuel II Palaiologos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manuel_II_Palaiologos) (r. 1391–1425) itself surrendered after a [lengthy siege in 1383–1387](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Thessalonica_\(1383%E2%80%931387\)), along with most of eastern and central Macedonia, to the forces of Sultan [Murad I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Murad_I).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVacalopoulos197359–64-80] Initially, the surrendered cities were allowed complete autonomy in exchange for payment of the *[kharaj](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kharaj)* [poll-tax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poll-tax). Following the death of Emperor [John V Palaiologos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_V_Palaiologos) in 1391, however, Manuel II escaped Ottoman custody and went to Constantinople, where he was crowned emperor, succeeding his father. This angered Sultan [Bayezid I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bayezid_I), who laid waste to the remaining Byzantine territories, and then turned on Chrysopolis, which was captured by storm and largely destroyed.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVacalopoulos197364–65-81] Thessalonica also submitted again to Ottoman rule at this time, possibly after brief resistance, but was treated more leniently: although the city was brought under full Ottoman control, the Christian population and the Church retained most of their possessions, and the city retained its institutions.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVacalopoulos197365–67-82][^cite_note-FOOTNOTENecipoğlu200930,_84–99-83]



A mosaic of [Saint George](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_George) in [Saint Demetrios Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hagios_Demetrios)

Thessalonica remained in Ottoman hands until 1403, when Emperor Manuel II sided with Bayezid's eldest son [Süleyman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S%C3%BCleyman_%C3%87elebi) in the [Ottoman succession struggle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_Interregnum) that broke out following the crushing defeat and capture of Bayezid at the [Battle of Ankara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Ankara) against [Tamerlane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tamerlane) in 1402. In exchange for his support, in the [Treaty of Gallipoli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Gallipoli) the Byzantine emperor secured the return of Thessalonica, part of its hinterland, the [Chalcidice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chalcidice) peninsula, and the coastal region between the rivers [Strymon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strymon_River) and [Pineios](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pineios_\(Thessaly\)).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVacalopoulos197367,_75-84][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBryer1998777–778-85] Thessalonica and the surrounding region were given as an autonomous [appanage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Appanage) to [John VII Palaiologos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_VII_Palaiologos). After his death in 1408, he was succeeded by Manuel's third son, the [Despot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Despot_\(court_title\)) [Andronikos Palaiologos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andronikos_Palaiologos_\(son_of_Manuel_II\)), who was supervised by [Demetrios Leontares](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demetrios_Leontares) until 1415.

Thessalonica enjoyed a period of relative peace and prosperity after 1403, as the Turks were preoccupied with [their own civil war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_Interregnum), but was attacked by the rival Ottoman pretenders in 1412 (by [Musa Çelebi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Musa_%C3%87elebi)[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMagoulias1975108-86]) and 1416 (during the uprising of [Mustafa Çelebi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mustafa_%C3%87elebi) against [Mehmed I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mehmed_I)[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMagoulias1975123–125-87]).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVacalopoulos197376–77-88][^cite_note-FOOTNOTENecipoğlu200939,_44,_47-89] Once the Ottoman civil war ended, the Turkish pressure on the city began to increase again. Just as during the 1383–1387 siege, this led to a sharp division of opinion within the city between factions supporting resistance, if necessary with Western help, or submission to the Ottomans.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENecipoğlu200946–47-90]

In 1423, Despot Andronikos Palaiologos ceded it to the [Republic of Venice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_of_Venice) with the hope that it could be protected from the Ottomans, who were [besieging the city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Thessalonica_\(1422%E2%80%931430\)). The Venetians held Thessaloniki until it was captured by the [Ottoman](22278.md) Sultan [Murad II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Murad_II) on 29 March 1430.[^cite_note-91] Thus began 500 years of Ottoman Turkish rule in Thessaloniki/Selânik, which would profoundly shape the city's unique multicultural character and urban architecture.

### Ottoman period

Further information: [Ottoman Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_Greece), [Salonica Eyalet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salonica_Eyalet), and [Salonica Vilayet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salonica_Vilayet)

See also: [Committee of Union and Progress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Committee_of_Union_and_Progress)



Hot chamber of the men's baths in the [Bey Hamam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bey_Hamam) (1444)

When Sultan [Murad II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Murad_II) [captured Thessaloniki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Thessalonica_\(1422%E2%80%931430\)) and [sacked](209811.md) it in 1430,[^cite_note-history_siege-92] contemporary reports estimated that about one-fifth of the city's population was enslaved.[^cite_note-93] [Ottoman artillery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_weapons#Firearms_and_artillery) was used to secure the city's capture and bypass its double walls.[^cite_note-history_siege-92] Upon the conquest of Thessaloniki, some of its inhabitants escaped,[^cite_note-94] including intellectuals such as [Theodorus Gaza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theodorus_Gaza) "Thessalonicensis" and [Andronicus Callistus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andronicus_Callistus).[^cite_note-95] However, the change of sovereignty from the Byzantine Empire to the Ottoman one did not affect the city's prestige as a major imperial city and trading hub.[^cite_note-Economic_History-96][^cite_note-Ottoman_Encyclopedia-97] Thessaloniki and [Smyrna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Smyrna), although smaller in size than [Constantinople](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constantinople), were the Ottoman Empire's most important trading hubs.[^cite_note-Economic_History-96] Thessaloniki's importance was mostly in the field of [shipping](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shipping),[^cite_note-Economic_History-96] but also in manufacturing,[^cite_note-Ottoman_Encyclopedia-97] while most of the city's tradespeople were [Jewish](25955086.md).[^cite_note-Economic_History-96]



Demographics of Thessaloniki between 1500 and 1950

[^cite_note-98]

During the Ottoman period, the city's population of [Ottoman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_Turks) [Muslims](19541.md) (including those of [Turkish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_people) origin, as well as [Albanian Muslim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albanian_Muslims), [Bulgarian Muslim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bulgarian_Muslim), especially the Pomaks and [Greek Muslim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Muslim) of convert origin) and Muslim Roma like the [Sepečides Romani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sepe%C4%8Dides_Romani) grew substantially. According to the 1478 census Selânik ([Ottoman Turkish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_Turkish_language): سلانیك), as the city came to be known in Ottoman Turkish, had 6,094 Christian Orthodox [households](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Household), 4,320 Muslim ones, and some Catholic. No Jews were recorded in the census, suggesting that the subsequent influx of Jewish population was not linked[^cite_note-99] to the already existing [Romaniote](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romaniote_Jews) community.[^cite_note-100]



The oldest surviving photograph of Thessaloniki (Selânik), taken in 1863 by Josef Székely from the area called Beş Çınar (Five Sycamores), a popular spot for panoramic vistas during the Ottoman era.

Soon after the turn of the 15th to 16th century, however, nearly 20,000 [Sephardic Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sephardic_Jews) immigrated to Greece from the [Iberian Peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iberian_Peninsula) following their expulsion from Spain by the 1492 [Alhambra Decree](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alhambra_Decree).[^cite_note-Sephardic_jews-101] By c. 1500, the number of Christians had grown to 7,986, the Muslims to 8,575, and the Jews to 3,770. In 1519, according to Ottoman archives, the population of Thessaloniki numbered 1,374 Muslim households and 282 bachelors, for a total of 6,870, 1,078 Christian households and 355 bachelors, for a total of about 6,635, and 3,143 Hebrew households with 530 bachelors, for a total of 15,715, 54% of the city's population.[^cite_note-102] Some historians consider the Ottoman regime's invitation to Jewish settlement was a strategy to prevent the Christian population from dominating the city.[^cite_note-103] The city became both the largest Jewish city in the world and the only Jewish majority city in the world in the 16th century. As a result, Thessaloniki attracted persecuted Jews from all over the world.[^cite_note-104]

Thessaloniki was the capital of the [Sanjak of Selanik](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanjak_of_Selanik) within the wider [Rumeli Eyalet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rumeli_Eyalet) (Balkans)[^cite_note-Society-105] until 1826, and subsequently the capital of [Selanik Eyalet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Selanik_Eyalet) (after 1867, the [Selanik Vilayet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Selanik_Vilayet)).[^cite_note-Studies-106][^cite_note-History_and_progress-107] This consisted of the sanjaks of Selanik, [Serres](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanjak_of_Serres) and [Drama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanjak_of_Drama) between 1826 and 1912.[^cite_note-La_Turquie-108]

With the break out of the [Greek War of Independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_War_of_Independence) in the spring of 1821, the governor Yusuf Bey imprisoned in his headquarters more than 400 hostages. On 18 May, when Yusuf learned of the insurrection to the villages of [Chalkidiki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chalkidiki), he ordered half of his hostages to be slaughtered before his eyes. The mulla of Thessaloniki, Hayrıülah, gives the following description of Yusuf's retaliations: "Every day and every night you hear nothing in the streets of Thessaloniki but shouting and moaning. It seems that Yusuf Bey, the Yeniceri Agasi, the Subaşı, the hocas and the ulemas have all gone raving mad."[^cite_note-109] It would take until the end of the century for the city's Greek community to recover.[^cite_note-110]



The [Yeni Mosque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yeni_Mosque,_Thessaloniki) (New Mosque), designed by architect [Vitaliano Poselli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vitaliano_Poselli), was built in 1902 in the [Turkish Neoclassical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_Neoclassical_architecture) style, with elements of [Baroque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baroque_architecture) and [Renaissance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renaissance_architecture) architecture. Variously repurposed throughout the 20th century, it was reopened for regular Muslim worship in 2024.

[^cite_note-111]

Thessaloniki was also a [Janissary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Janissary) stronghold where novice Janissaries were trained. In June 1826, regular Ottoman soldiers attacked and destroyed the Janissary base in Thessaloniki while also killing over 10,000 Janissaries, an event known as [The Auspicious Incident](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Auspicious_Incident) in Ottoman history.[^cite_note-Janissary-112] In 1870–1917, driven by economic growth, the city's population expanded by 70%, reaching 135,000 in 1917.[^cite_note-Gerolympos-113]

The last few decades of Ottoman control over the city were an era of revival, particularly in terms of the city's infrastructure. It was at that time that the Ottoman administration of the city acquired an "official" face with the creation of the [Government House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_House_\(Thessaloniki\))[^cite_note-Thessaloniki_En_Thermo_Kanaki-114] while a number of new public buildings were built in the [eclectic style](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eclecticism) in order to project the European face both of Thessaloniki and the Ottoman Empire.[^cite_note-Thessaloniki_En_Thermo_Kanaki-114][^cite_note-Thessaloniki_En_Thermo_Eklektismos-115] The city walls were torn down between 1869 and 1889,[^cite_note-Thessaloniki_En_Thermo_Toixi-116] efforts for a planned expansion of the city are evident as early as 1879,[^cite_note-Thessaloniki_En_Thermo_Sidirodromos-117] the first tram service started in 1888[^cite_note-Thessaloniki_En_Thermo_Tram-118] and the city streets were illuminated with electric lamp posts in 1908.[^cite_note-Thessaloniki_En_Thermo_Ilektrismos-119] In 1888, the [Oriental Railway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chemins_de_fer_Orientaux) connected Thessaloniki to Central Europe via rail through [Belgrade](55904.md) and to [Monastir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bitola) in 1893, while the [Thessaloniki–Istanbul Junction Railway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thessaloniki%E2%80%93Alexandroupoli_railway) connected it to [Constantinople](3391396.md) in 1896.[^cite_note-Thessaloniki_En_Thermo_Sidirodromos-117]

[Mustafa Kemal Atatürk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mustafa_Kemal_Atat%C3%BCrk), founder of the modern republic of [Turkey](11125639.md), was born in Thessaloniki (then known as Selânik in [Ottoman Turkish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_Turkish)) in 1881. His birthplace on İslahhane Caddesi (now 17 Apostolou Street) is now the [Atatürk Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atat%C3%BCrk_Museum_\(Thessaloniki\)) and forms part of the Turkish consulate complex.[^cite_note-120]

Other notable figures in Turkish and Ottoman history who were born in the city during the Ottoman era include poet and playwright [Nâzım Hikmet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/N%C3%A2z%C4%B1m_Hikmet), Turkey's first female journalist [Sabiha Sertel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sabiha_Sertel), the Ottoman chronicler [Mustafa Selaniki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mustafa_Selaniki), Turkish patriot [Hasan Tahsin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hasan_Tahsin) (no relation to [Hasan Tahsin Pasha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hasan_Tahsin_Pasha)), and Young Turk politician [Mehmed Cavid Bey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mehmed_Cavid_Bey).

### 20th century and beyond

See also: [Balkan Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balkan_Wars), [Macedonian front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Macedonian_front), [Provisional Government of National Defence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Provisional_Government_of_National_Defence), [Axis occupation of Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Axis_occupation_of_Greece), and [History of the Jews in Thessaloniki § Destruction of the Jews of Salonika](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Jews_in_Thessaloniki#Destruction_of_the_Jews_of_Salonika)



Painting of the seafront of Thessaloniki in 1917

In the early 20th century, Thessaloniki was in the centre of radical activities by various groups; the [Internal Macedonian Revolutionary Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Internal_Macedonian_Revolutionary_Organization), founded in 1897,[^cite_note-IMRO-121] and the Greek [Macedonian Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Struggle_for_Macedonia#Causes), founded in 1903.[^cite_note-Macedonian_Committee-122] In 1903, a [Bulgarian anarchist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anarchism_in_Bulgaria) group known as the [Boatmen of Thessaloniki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boatmen_of_Thessaloniki) planted bombs in several buildings in Thessaloniki, including the [Ottoman Bank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_Bank), with some assistance from the IMRO. The Greek consulate in Ottoman Thessaloniki (now the [Museum of the Macedonian Struggle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Museum_for_the_Macedonian_Struggle_\(Thessaloniki\))) served as the centre of operations for the Greek guerillas.

During this period, and since the 16th century, Thessaloniki's Jewish element was the most dominant; it was the only city in Europe where the Jews were a majority of the total population.[^cite_note-executive_summary-123] The city was ethnically diverse and [cosmopolitan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cosmopolitanism). In 1890, its population had risen to 118,000, 47% of which were Jews, followed by Turks (22%), Greeks (14%), Bulgarians (8%), Roma (2%), and others (7%).[^cite_note-20thcen-124] By 1913, the ethnic composition of the city had changed so that the population stood at 157,889, with Jews at 39%, followed again by Turks (29%), Greeks (25%), Bulgarians (4%), Roma (2%), and others at 1%.[^cite_note-19thcen-125] Many varied religions were practiced and many languages spoken, including [Judeo-Spanish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judaeo-Spanish), a dialect of Spanish spoken by the city's Jews.



[George I of Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_I_of_Greece) with [Crown Prince Constantine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constantine_I_of_Greece) and the Greek army enter the city

Thessaloniki was also the centre of activities of the [Young Turks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Young_Turks), a political reform movement, whose goal was to replace the Ottoman Empire's absolute monarchy with a constitutional government. The Young Turks started out as an underground movement, until finally in 1908, they started the [Young Turk Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Young_Turk_Revolution) from the city of Thessaloniki, which led to them gaining control over the Ottoman Empire and put an end to the Ottoman sultan's power.[^cite_note-Young_Turk-126] [Eleftherias (Liberty) Square](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eleftherias_Square), where the Young Turks gathered at the outbreak of the revolution, is named after the event.[^cite_note-127] Turkey's first president [Mustafa Kemal Atatürk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mustafa_Kemal_Atat%C3%BCrk), who was born and raised in Thessaloniki, was a member of the Young Turks in his soldier days and also partook in the Young Turk Revolution.



[Allied](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allies_of_World_War_I) armies in Thessaloniki, World War I



The 1st Battalion of the [Army of National Defence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Army_of_National_Defence) marches on its way to the [Macedonian front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Macedonian_front).

#### Balkan Wars and the end of Ottoman rule

Main article: [Greek capture of Thessaloniki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_capture_of_Thessaloniki)

As the [First Balkan War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Balkan_War) broke out, Greece declared war on the Ottoman Empire and expanded its borders. When [Eleftherios Venizelos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eleftherios_Venizelos), [Prime Minister](273647.md) at the time, was asked if the Greek army should move towards Thessaloniki or Monastir (now [Bitola](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bitola), [Republic of North Macedonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_of_North_Macedonia)), Venizelos replied "Θεσσαλονίκη με κάθε κόστος!" (*Thessaloniki, at all costs!*).[^cite_note-Chester-128] With the outnumbered Ottoman Army fighting a rearguard action against well-prepared Greek forces at [Yenidje](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Yenidje), Bulgarian troops advancing close by, and the Ottoman naval base at Thessaloniki blockaded by the Greek Navy, General [Hasan Tahsin Pasha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hasan_Tahsin_Pasha) soon realised that it had become untenable to defend the city. The sinking of the Ottoman ironclad *[Feth-i Bülend](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Feth-i_B%C3%BClend)* in Thessaloniki harbour on 31 October \[O.S. 18 October] 1912, although militarily negligible, further damaged Ottoman morale.

As both Greece and [Bulgaria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Bulgaria) wanted Thessaloniki, the Ottoman garrison of the city entered into negotiations with both armies separately.[^cite_note-Prelude-129] Ultimately it was decided to hand over the city to the Greeks, in part because there was a perception on the Ottoman side that the Greek troops would be more lenient towards its residents. Negotiations between the Greek and Turkish delegations were concluded at the village of [Topçin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gefyra,_Thessaloniki) on the outskirts of the city. On 8 November 1912 (26 October [Old Style](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adoption_of_the_Gregorian_calendar)), the feast day of the city's patron saint, [Saint Demetrius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_Demetrius_of_Thessaloniki), the Greek Army accepted the peaceful and unconditional surrender of the 25,000-strong Ottoman garrison at Thessaloniki, bringing almost five centuries of Ottoman rule to an end.[^cite_note-26_October-130] The Bulgarian army arrived one day after the surrender of the city to Greece and Hasan Tahsin Pasha, commander of the city's defences, told the Bulgarian officials that "I have only one Thessaloniki, which I have surrendered".[^cite_note-Prelude-129]

After the [Second Balkan War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Balkan_War), Thessaloniki and the rest of the Greek portion of Macedonia were officially annexed to Greece by the [Treaty of Bucharest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Bucharest_\(1913\)) in 1913.[^cite_note-Traty_of_Bucharest-131] On 18 March 1913 [George I of Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_I_of_Greece) was assassinated in the city by [Alexandros Schinas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexandros_Schinas).[^cite_note-Assassination-132]

#### World War I, the Great Fire, and population exchange

In 1915, during [World War I](4764461.md), a large [Allied](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allies_of_World_War_I) [expeditionary force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Expeditionary_warfare) established a base at Thessaloniki for operations[^cite_note-133] against pro-German Bulgaria.[^cite_note-Entente_in_Thessaloniki-134] This culminated in the establishment of the [Macedonian Front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Macedonian_front_\(World_War_I\)), also known as the Salonika front,[^cite_note-Salonika_front-135][^cite_note-136] and a temporary hospital run by the [Scottish Women's Hospitals for Foreign Service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_Women's_Hospitals_for_Foreign_Service) being set up in a disused factory. In 1916, pro-[Venizelist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Venizelism) Greek army officers and civilians, with the support of the Allies, launched an uprising,[^cite_note-Mavrogordatos-137] creating a pro-Allied[^cite_note-Minorities-138] [temporary government](598010.md) by the name of the "[Provisional Government of National Defence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Provisional_Government_of_National_Defence)"[^cite_note-Mavrogordatos-137][^cite_note-Venizelos-139] that controlled the "New Lands" (lands that were gained by Greece in the [Balkan Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balkan_Wars), most of Northern Greece including Greek Macedonia, the [North Aegean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Aegean) as well as the island of [Crete](6591.md));[^cite_note-Mavrogordatos-137][^cite_note-Venizelos-139] the official government of the King in [Athens](1216.md), called in historiography as the "State of Athens",[^cite_note-Mavrogordatos-137] controlled "[Old Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Greece)"[^cite_note-Mavrogordatos-137][^cite_note-Venizelos-139] which was traditionally monarchist. The State of Thessaloniki was disestablished with the unification of the two opposing Greek governments under Venizelos, following the [abdication](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdicate) of [King Constantine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constantine_I_of_Greece) in 1917.[^cite_note-Entente_in_Thessaloniki-134][^cite_note-Venizelos-139]

On 30 December 1915 an [Austrian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Austria-Hungarian_Empire) [air raid](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/air%20raid) on Thessaloniki alarmed many town civilians and killed at least one person, and in response the Allied troops based there arrested the German, Austrian, Bulgarian and Turkish [vice-consuls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vice-consul) and their families and dependents and put them on a battleship, and billeted troops in their consulate buildings in Thessaloniki.[^cite_note-140]



Aerial photograph of the [Great Fire of 1917](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Thessaloniki_Fire_of_1917)

Most of the old centre of the city was destroyed by the [Great Thessaloniki Fire of 1917](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Thessaloniki_Fire_of_1917), which was started accidentally by an unattended kitchen fire on 18 August 1917.[^cite_note-Redesign-141] The fire swept through the centre of the city, leaving 72,000 people homeless; according to the Pallis Report, most of them were Jewish (50,000). Many businesses were destroyed, as a result, 70% of the population were unemployed.[^cite_note-Redesign-141] Two churches and many synagogues and mosques were lost. More than one quarter of the total population of approximately 271,157 became homeless.[^cite_note-Redesign-141] Following the fire the government prohibited quick rebuilding, so it could implement the new redesign of the city according to the European-style urban plan[^cite_note-Neoclassical-10] prepared by a group of architects, including the Briton [Thomas Mawson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Mawson), and headed by French architect [Ernest Hébrard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ernest_H%C3%A9brard).[^cite_note-Redesign-141] Property values fell from 6.5 million [Greek drachmas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modern_drachma) to 750,000.[^cite_note-History-142]

After the defeat of Greece in the [Greco-Turkish War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greco-Turkish_War_\(1919%E2%80%931922\)) and during the break-up of the Ottoman Empire, a [population exchange](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Population_exchange_between_Greece_and_Turkey) took place between Greece and Turkey.[^cite_note-Minorities-138] Over 160,000 ethnic [Greeks deported](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_refugees) from the former [Ottoman Empire](22278.md) – particularly Greeks from [Asia Minor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asia_Minor)[^cite_note-143] and [East Thrace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Thrace) were resettled in the city,[^cite_note-Minorities-138] changing its demographics. Additionally many of the city's Muslims, including Ottoman [Greek Muslims](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Muslims), were deported to Turkey, ranging at about 20,000 people.[^cite_note-Exchange-144] This made the Greek element dominant,[^cite_note-145] while the Jewish population was reduced to a minority for the first time since the 16th century.[^cite_note-146] This was part of an overall process of modern Hellenization, which affected nearly all minorities within Greece, turning the region into a hotspot of ethnic nationalism.[^cite_note-147]



Dead protester during the tobacco strike in May 1936

In May 1936, a massive strike by tobacco workers led to general anarchy in the city and [Ioannis Metaxas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ioannis_Metaxas) (future dictator, then PM) ordered its repression. The events and the deaths of the protesters inspired [Yiannis Ritsos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yiannis_Ritsos) to write the *Epitafios*.



Registration of the male [Jews of Thessaloniki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Jews_in_Thessaloniki) in July 1942, [Eleftherias Square](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eleftherias_Square). 96% of deported Jews perished in [Nazi concentration camps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nazi_concentration_camps).

[^cite_note-148]

#### World War II



Part of Eleftherias Square and [Stein mansion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stein_mansion) during the [Axis occupation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Axis_occupation_of_Greece)

During [World War II](32927.md) Thessaloniki was heavily bombarded by [Fascist Italy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Italy#Fascist_regime_\(1922%E2%80%931943\)) (with 232 people dead, 871 wounded and over 800 buildings damaged or destroyed in November 1940 alone),[^cite_note-Thessaloniki_En_Thermo_Italia-149] and, the Italians having failed in [their invasion of Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greco-Italian_War), it fell to the forces of [Nazi Germany](21212.md) on 8 April 1941[^cite_note-Chronology-150] and went under German occupation. The Nazis soon forced the Jewish residents into a ghetto near the railroads and on 15 March 1943 began the deportation of the city's Jews to [Auschwitz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Auschwitz_concentration_camp) and [Bergen-Belsen concentration camps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bergen-Belsen_concentration_camp).[^cite_note-151][^cite_note-Holocaust-152][^cite_note-Exodus-153] Most were immediately murdered in the [gas chambers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gas_chambers). Of the 45,000 Jews deported to Auschwitz, only 4% survived.[^cite_note-154][^cite_note-155]



Indian troops sweep for mines in Salonika, 1944

Speaking in the [Reichstag](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reichstag_\(Nazi_Germany\)), Hitler claimed that the intention of his Balkan campaign was to prevent the Allies from establishing "a new Macedonian front", as they had during WWI. The importance of Thessaloniki to Nazi Germany can be demonstrated by the fact that, initially, [Hitler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hitler) had planned to incorporate it directly into Nazi Germany[^cite_note-Thessaloniki_En_Thermo_Trito_Raich-156] and not have it controlled by a puppet state such as the [Hellenic State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hellenic_State_\(1941%E2%80%931944\)) or an ally of Germany (Thessaloniki had been promised to [Yugoslavia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Yugoslavia) as a reward for joining the [Axis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Axis_powers) on 25 March 1941).[^cite_note-Thessaloniki_En_Thermo_Yugoslavia-157]

As it was the first major city in Greece to fall to the occupying forces, the first [Greek resistance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_resistance) group was formed in Thessaloniki (under the name Ελευθερία, *Elefthería*, "Freedom")[^cite_note-Thessaloniki_En_Thermo_Eleftheria-158] as well as the first anti-Nazi newspaper in an occupied territory anywhere in Europe,[^cite_note-Thessaloniki_En_Thermo_Eleftheria_2-159] also by the name *Eleftheria*. Thessaloniki was also home to a military camp-converted-concentration camp, known in German as "Konzentrationslager Pavlo Mela" ([Pavlos Melas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pavlos_Melas) Concentration Camp),[^cite_note-Thessaloniki_En_Thermo_Pavlos_Melas-160] where members of the resistance and other anti-fascists[^cite_note-Thessaloniki_En_Thermo_Pavlos_Melas-160] were held either to be killed or sent to other concentration camps.[^cite_note-Thessaloniki_En_Thermo_Pavlos_Melas-160] In September 1943, the Germans established the Dulag 410 transit camp for [Italian Military Internees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italian_Military_Internees) in the city.[^cite_note-161] On 30 October 1944, after battles with the retreating German army and the Security Battalions of [Poulos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgios_Poulos), forces of [ELAS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_People's_Liberation_Army) entered Thessaloniki as liberators headed by [Markos Vafiadis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Markos_Vafiadis) (who did not obey orders from ELAS leadership in Athens to not enter the city). Pro-EAM celebrations and demonstrations followed in the city.[^cite_note-162][^cite_note-Crossroads-163] In the [1946 monarchy referendum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_plebiscite,_1946), the majority of the locals voted in favor of a republic, contrary to the rest of Greece.[^cite_note-Thessaloniki_En_Thermo_Referendum-164]

#### Post-war: contemporary Thessaloniki

After the war, Thessaloniki was rebuilt with large-scale development of new infrastructure and industry throughout the 1950s, 1960s and 1970s. Many of its architectural treasures still remain, adding value to the city as a tourist destination, while several early Christian and Byzantine monuments of Thessaloniki were added to the [UNESCO](21786641.md) [World Heritage list](44940.md) in 1988.[^cite_note-UNESCO-165] In 1997, Thessaloniki was celebrated as the [European Capital of Culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Capital_of_Culture),[^cite_note-Capital_of_Culture-166] sponsoring events across the city and the region. Agency established to oversee the cultural activities of that year 1997 was still in existence by 2010.[^cite_note-isbn0-85728-771-0-167] In 2004, the city hosted a number of the [football](10568.md) events as part of the [2004 Summer Olympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2004_Summer_Olympics).[^cite_note-Athens_2004-168]

Today, Thessaloniki has become one of the most important trade and business hubs in [Southeastern Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southeastern_Europe), with its port, the [Port of Thessaloniki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Thessaloniki) being one of the largest in the Aegean and facilitating trade throughout the Balkan hinterland.[^cite_note-Development-11] On 26 October 2012 the city celebrated its centennial since its incorporation into Greece.[^cite_note-100_years-169] The city also forms one of the largest student centers in Southeastern Europe, is host to the largest student population in Greece and was the [European Youth Capital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Youth_Capital) in 2014.[^cite_note-Thessaloniki_EYC-170][^cite_note-Thessaloniki_2014-171] Infrastructure improvements came in the 2020s with the upgrade and expansion of [Thessaloniki Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thessaloniki_Airport) in 2021 and the opening of [Thessaloniki Metro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thessaloniki_Metro) in 2024.

## Geography

Thessaloniki is located 502 kilometres (312 mi) north of [Athens](1216.md).

Thessaloniki's urban area spreads over 30 kilometres (19 mi) from [Oraiokastro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oraiokastro) in the north to [Thermi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thermi) in the south in the direction of [Chalkidiki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chalkidiki).

### Geology

Thessaloniki lies on the northern fringe of the [Thermaic Gulf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thermaic_Gulf) on its eastern coast and is bound by [Mount Chortiatis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_Chortiatis) on its southeast. Its proximity to imposing [mountain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mountain) ranges, hills and fault lines, especially towards its southeast have historically made the city prone to geological changes.

Since medieval times, Thessaloniki has been hit by strong [earthquakes](10106.md), notably in 1759, 1902, 1978 and 1995.[^cite_note-Thessaloniki_earthquakes-172] On 19–20 June 1978, the city suffered [a series of powerful earthquakes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1978_Thessaloniki_earthquake), registering 5.5 and 6.5 on the [Richter scale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richter_magnitude_scale).[^cite_note-USGS-173][^cite_note-Maniateas-174] The tremors caused considerable damage to a number of buildings and ancient monuments,[^cite_note-USGS-173] but the city withstood the catastrophe without any major problems.[^cite_note-Maniateas-174] One apartment building in central Thessaloniki collapsed during the second earthquake, killing many and raising the final death toll to 51.[^cite_note-USGS-173][^cite_note-Maniateas-174]



Panoramic view of the city from [Kedrinos Lofos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kedrinos_Lofos) with [Mount Olympus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_Olympus) in the background

### Climate

Thessaloniki's climate is transitional, lying on the periphery of multiple climate zones. According to the [Köppen climate classification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C3%B6ppen_climate_classification), the city generally has a [cold semi-arid climate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cold_semi-arid_climate) (*BSk*) while a [hot semi-arid climate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hot_semi-arid_climate) (*BSh*) is found in the center. [Mediterranean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mediterranean_climate) (*Csa*) and [humid subtropical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Humid_subtropical_climate) (*Cfa*) influences are also found in the city's climate.[^cite_note-175][^cite_note-176] Like in [Athens](1216.md) the [Pindus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pindus) mountain range greatly contributes to the generally dry climate of the area by substantially drying the westerly winds.[^cite_note-177] In fact, the [Thessaloniki International Fair](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thessaloniki_International_Fair) station of the [National Observatory of Athens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Observatory_of_Athens) is the northernmost station in the world with a hot semi-arid climate (*BSh*).[^cite_note-178]

Winters are somewhat dry, with occasional morning frost. Snowfalls occur more or less every winter, but the snow cover does not last for more than a few days. During the coldest winters, temperatures can drop to −10 °C (14 °F).[^cite_note-Weatherbase-179] The record minimum temperature in Thessaloniki was −14 °C (7 °F).[^cite_note-HNMS-180] On average, Thessaloniki experiences frost (sub-zero temperature) 32 days a year,[^cite_note-Weatherbase-179] though that's less common near the city centre, owing to the [urban heat island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urban_heat_island) effect which characterizes the city and is more pronounced during the winter months.[^cite_note-181] Foggy days occur sparsely, roughly 17 days a year, mainly in the autumn and winter months.[^cite_note-182] The coldest month of the year in downtown Thessaloniki is January, with an average 24-hour temperature of 8 °C (46 °F).[^cite_note-auto-183] The city is also quite windy in the winter months, with January and February having an average wind speed of about 11 km/h (7 mph).[^cite_note-Weatherbase-179]

Thessaloniki's summers are hot and moderately dry.[^cite_note-Weatherbase-179] Maximum temperatures usually rise above 30 °C (86 °F),[^cite_note-Weatherbase-179] but they rarely exceed 40 °C (104 °F);[^cite_note-Weatherbase-179] while the average number of days the temperature is above 32 °C (90 °F) is 32.[^cite_note-Weatherbase-179] Generally, the [sea breeze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sea_breeze) blowing from the [Thermaic gulf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thermaic_Gulf) helps moderate the city's temperatures.[^cite_note-184] The maximum recorded temperature in the city was 44 °C (111 °F).[^cite_note-Weatherbase-179][^cite_note-HNMS-180] Rain occasionally falls in the summer, mainly during thunderstorms, while [heat waves](293270.md) occur sporadically, though few of them are intense.[^cite_note-Climate,_change_and_risk-185] The hottest months of the year in downtown Thessaloniki are July and August, with an average 24-hour temperature of around 28.0 °C (82 °F).[^cite_note-auto-183]

In 2021, Greece was taken to task by the European Commission for failing to curb consistently high [air pollution](10934212.md) levels in Thessaloniki.[^cite_note-186]

## Government



Thessaloniki's [urban](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thessaloniki_Urban_Area) and [metropolitan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thessaloniki_metropolitan_area) areas as of 2011

According to the [Kallikratis reform](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kallikratis_reform), as of 1 January 2011 the [Thessaloniki Urban Area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thessaloniki_Urban_Area) ([Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_language): Πολεοδομικό Συγκρότημα Θεσσαλονίκης) which makes up the "City of Thessaloniki", is made up of six self-governing [municipalities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_municipalities_of_Greece) ([Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_language): Δήμοι) and one municipal unit ([Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_language): Δημοτική ενότητα). The municipalities that are included in the Thessaloniki Urban Area are those of Thessaloniki (the city centre and largest in population size), [Kalamaria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kalamaria), [Neapoli-Sykies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neapoli-Sykies), [Pavlos Melas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pavlos_Melas_\(municipality\)), [Kordelio-Evosmos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kordelio-Evosmos), [Ampelokipoi-Menemeni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ampelokipoi-Menemeni), and the municipal units of [Pylaia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pylaia) and [Panorama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Panorama,_Thessaloniki), part of the municipality of [Pylaia-Chortiatis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pylaia-Chortiatis).[^cite_note-urban_metro-4] Prior to the Kallikratis reform, the Thessaloniki Urban Area was made up of twice as many municipalities, considerably smaller in size, which created bureaucratic problems.[^cite_note-197]

### Thessaloniki Municipality

See also: [List of mayors of Thessaloniki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_mayors_of_Thessaloniki)

The [municipality of Thessaloniki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thessaloniki_\(municipality\)) ([Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_language): Δήμος Θεσαλονίκης) is the second most populous in Greece, after [Athens](1216.md), with a resident population of 317,778[^cite_note-2021census-3] (in 2021) and an area of 19.307 square kilometres (7.454 square miles). The municipality forms the core of the [Thessaloniki Urban Area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thessaloniki_Urban_Area), with its central district (the city centre), referred to as the *Kentro*, meaning 'centre' or 'downtown'.[^cite_note-198] The municipal flag of Thessaloniki includes a stylized depiction of the city's main landmark, the [White Tower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_Tower_of_Thessaloniki), and an [ancient Macedonian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Macedonians) coin depicting [Alexander the Great](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_the_Great), who is also depicted in its seal and logo.

The city's first mayor, Osman Sait Bey, was appointed when the institution of mayor was inaugurated under the [Ottoman Empire](22278.md) in 1912. The incumbent mayor is [Stelios Angeloudis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stelios_Angeloudis?action=edit\&redlink=1). In 2011, the municipality of Thessaloniki had a budget of €464.33 million[^cite_note-Budget_2011-199] while the budget of 2012 stands at €409.00 million.[^cite_note-Budget_2012-200]

### Other

See also: [Ministry of Macedonia and Thrace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Macedonia_and_Thrace) and [Thessaloniki A (Hellenic Parliament constituency)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thessaloniki_A_\(Hellenic_Parliament_constituency\))



The [Government House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Konak_\(Thessaloniki\)), now the [Ministry for Macedonia and Thrace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_for_Macedonia_and_Thrace), designed by [Vitaliano Poselli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vitaliano_Poselli) in 1891



The Prefecture building ([Villa Allatini](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Villa_Allatini))

Thessaloniki is the second largest city in Greece. It is an influential city for the northern parts of the country and is the capital of the [region of Central Macedonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Macedonia) and the [Thessaloniki regional unit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thessaloniki_\(regional_unit\)). The Ministry of Macedonia and Thrace is also based in Thessaloniki, since the city is the *de facto* capital of the [Greek region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geographic_regions_of_Greece) of [Macedonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Macedonia_\(Greece\)).

It is customary every year for the [Prime Minister of Greece](273647.md) to announce his administration's policies on a number of issues, such as the economy, at the opening night of the [Thessaloniki International Fair](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thessaloniki_International_Fair). In 2010, during the first months of the [2010 Greek debt crisis](12113.md#2010_debt_crisis), the entire [cabinet of Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cabinet_of_Greece) met in Thessaloniki to discuss the country's future.[^cite_note-201]

In the [Hellenic Parliament](2449826.md), the Thessaloniki urban area constitutes a 17-seat constituency. As of the [June 2023 Greek legislative election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/June_2023_Greek_legislative_election) the largest party in Thessaloniki is the [New Democracy](424225.md) with 35.28% of the vote, followed by [Syriza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syriza) (17.52%).[^cite_note-Elections_September_2019-202] The table below summarizes the results of the latest elections.
