## Cityscape



Plan for central Thessaloniki by [Ernest Hébrard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ernest_H%C3%A9brard). Much of the plan can be seen in today's city centre.

### Architecture

Architecture in Thessaloniki is the direct result of the city's position at the centre of all historical developments in the Balkans. Aside from its commercial importance, Thessaloniki was also for many centuries the military and administrative hub of the region, and beyond this the transportation link between Europe and [the Levant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Levant). Merchants, traders and refugees from all over Europe settled in the city. The need for commercial and public buildings in this new era of prosperity led to the construction of large edifices in the city centre. During this time, the city saw the building of banks, large hotels, theatres, warehouses, and factories. Architects who designed some of the most notable buildings of the city, in the late 19th and early 20th century, include [Vitaliano Poselli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vitaliano_Poselli), [Pietro Arrigoni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pietro_Arrigoni), [Xenophon Paionidis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xenophon_Paionidis), [Salvatore Poselli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salvatore_Poselli?action=edit\&redlink=1), [Leonardo Gennari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leonardo_Gennari?action=edit\&redlink=1), [Eli Modiano](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eli_Modiano?action=edit\&redlink=1), [Jacques Moshé](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacques_Mosh%C3%A9?action=edit\&redlink=1), [Joseph Pleyber](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_Pleyber?action=edit\&redlink=1), [Ernst Ziller](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ernst_Ziller), [Max Rubens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Max_Rubens?action=edit\&redlink=1), [Filimon Paionidis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Filimon_Paionidis?action=edit\&redlink=1), [Angelos Siagas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Angelos_Siagas?action=edit\&redlink=1), [Alexandros Tzonis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexandros_Tzonis?action=edit\&redlink=1) and more, using mainly the styles of [Eclecticism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eclecticism), [Art Nouveau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Art_Nouveau) and [Neobaroque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baroque_Revival_architecture).

The city layout changed after 1870, when the seaside fortifications gave way to extensive piers, and many of the oldest walls of the city were demolished, including those surrounding the [White Tower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_Tower_of_Thessaloniki), which today stands as the main landmark of the city. As parts of the early Byzantine walls were demolished, this allowed the city to expand east and west along the coast.[^cite_note-203]

The expansion of [Eleftherias Square](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eleftherias_Square) towards the sea completed the new commercial hub of the city and at the time was considered one of the most vibrant squares of the city. As the city grew, workers moved to the western districts, because of their proximity to factories and industrial activities; while the middle and [upper classes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_class) gradually moved from the city-centre to the eastern suburbs, leaving mainly businesses. In 1917, a devastating fire swept through the city and burned uncontrollably for 32 hours.[^cite_note-Gerolympos-113] It destroyed the city's historic centre and a large part of its architectural heritage, but paved the way for modern development featuring wider diagonal avenues and monumental squares.[^cite_note-Gerolympos-113][^cite_note-Gerolympos2-204]



Panoramic view of [Aristotelous Square](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aristotelous_Square), one of Thessaloniki's most recognizable areas, which was designed by [Ernest Hébrard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ernest_H%C3%A9brard)

### City centre

See also: [Great Thessaloniki Fire of 1917](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Thessaloniki_Fire_of_1917)



The old *Hotel Astoria* on [Tsimiski Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tsimiski_Street), typical [beaux-arts architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beaux-arts_architecture) of the post-fire architecture boom

After the Great Thessaloniki Fire of 1917, a team of architects and urban planners including [Thomas Mawson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Mawson) and [Ernest Hebrard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ernest_Hebrard), a French architect, chose the [Byzantine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_Empire) era as the basis of their (re)building designs for Thessaloniki's city centre. The new city plan included axes, diagonal streets and monumental squares, with a [street grid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grid_plan) that would channel traffic smoothly. The plan of 1917 included provisions for future population expansions and a street and road network that would be, and still is sufficient today.[^cite_note-Gerolympos-113] It contained sites for public buildings and provided for the restoration of Byzantine churches and Ottoman mosques.



A street in [Ladadika](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ladadika) district

Also called the *historic centre*, it is divided into several districts, including Dimokratias Square (Democracy Sq. known also as *Vardaris*) *[Ladadika](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ladadika)* (where many entertainment venues and tavernas are located), *Kapani* (where the [city's central Modiano market](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modiano_Market) is located), *Diagonios*, *[Navarinou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Navarinou_Square)*, *[Rotonda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arch_of_Galerius_and_Rotunda)*, *[Agia Sofia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agias_Sofias_Square)* and *Hippodromio*, which are all located around Thessaloniki's most central point, [Aristotelous Square](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aristotelous_Square).

Various commercial stoas around Aristotelous are named from the city's past and historic personalities of the city, like stoa [Hirsch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maurice_de_Hirsch), stoa [Carasso](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carasso_family)/Ermou, Pelosov, Colombou, Levi, [Modiano](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modiano_Market), Morpurgo, Mordoch, Simcha, Kastoria, Malakopi, Olympios, Emboron, Rogoti, Vyzantio, Tatti, Agiou Mina, Karipi etc.[^cite_note-205]

The western portion of the city centre is home to Thessaloniki's law courts, its [central international railway station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_railway_station_\(Thessaloniki\)) and the [port](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Thessaloniki), while its eastern side hosts the city's two universities, the [Thessaloniki International Exhibition Centre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thessaloniki_International_Fair), the city's [main stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kaftanzoglio_Stadium), its archaeological and Byzantine museums, the new city hall and its central parks and gardens, namely those of the *ΧΑΝΘ* and *Pedion tou Areos*.

### Ano Poli

Main article: [Upper Town (Thessaloniki)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Upper_Town_\(Thessaloniki\))

Ano Poli (also called *Old Town* and literally the *Upper Town*) is the heritage-listed district north of Thessaloniki's city centre that was not engulfed by the [great fire of 1917](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Thessaloniki_Fire_of_1917) and was declared a [UNESCO](21786641.md) [World Heritage Site](44940.md) through the ministerial actions of [Melina Merkouri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Melina_Merkouri), during the 1980s. It is the most traditional part of the city, still featuring small stone-paved streets, old squares and homes featuring old [Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_architecture) and [Ottoman architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_architecture). It is the favourite area of Thessaloniki's poets, intellectuals and bohemians.



Panorama of the city from [Ano Poli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Upper_Town_\(Thessaloniki\))

Ano Poli is also the highest point in Thessaloniki and as such, is the location of the city's acropolis, its Byzantine fort, the [Heptapyrgion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heptapyrgion_\(Thessaloniki\)), a large portion of the [city's remaining walls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walls_of_Thessaloniki), and with many of its additional Ottoman and Byzantine structures still standing. With the capture of Thessaloniki by the Ottomans in 1430, after a lengthy [siege](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Thessalonica_\(1422%E2%80%931430\)) of the city from 1422 to 1430, the Ottomans settled in Ano Poli. This geographical choice was attributed to the higher level of Ano Poli, which was convenient to control the rest of the population remotely, and the microclimate of the area, which favoured better living conditions in terms of hygiene compared to the areas of the centre.

Today, the area provides access to the *[Seich Sou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kedrinos_Lofos)* Forest National Park[^cite_note-206] and features panoramic views of the whole city and the [Thermaic Gulf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thermaic_Gulf). On clear days [Mount Olympus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_Olympus), at about 100 km (62 mi) away across the gulf, can also be seen towering the horizon.

### Other districts of Thessaloniki Municipality

In the Municipality of Thessaloniki, in addition to the historic centre and the Upper Town, are included the following districts: Xirokrini, Dikastiria (Courts), Ichthioskala, Palaios Stathmos, Lachanokipoi, Behtsinari, Panagia Faneromeni, Doxa, Saranta Ekklisies, Evangelistria, Triandria, Agia Triada-Faliro, Ippokrateio, Charilaou, Analipsi, Depot and Toumba.

In the area of the Old Railway Station (Palaios Stathmos) began the construction of the [Holocaust Museum of Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holocaust_Museum_of_Greece).[^cite_note-207][^cite_note-efsyn.gr-208] In this area are located the [Railway Museum of Thessaloniki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Railway_Museum_of_Thessaloniki), the [Water Supply Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Water_Supply_Museum) and large entertainment venues of the city, such as *Milos*, *Fix*, *Vilka* (which are housed in converted old factories). The [Thessaloniki railway station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thessaloniki_railway_station) is located on Monastiriou street.

Other extended and densely built-up residential areas are [Charilaou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charilaou) and [Toumba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Toumba_\(Thessaloniki\)), which is divided into "Ano Toumpa" and "Kato Toumpa". Toumba was named after the homonymous hill of Toumba, where extensive archaeological research takes place. It was created by refugees after the 1922 Asia Minor disaster and the population exchange (1923–24). On *Exochon* avenue (*Rue des Campagnes*, today Vasilissis Olgas and Vasileos Georgiou Avenues), was up until the 1920s home to the city's most affluent residents and formed the outermost suburbs of the city at the time, with the area close to the [Thermaic Gulf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thermaic_Gulf), from the 19th-century holiday villas which defined the area.[^cite_note-209][^cite_note-210]

### Thessaloniki urban area



The cultural centre (including [MOMus–Museum of Modern Art–Costakis Collection](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MOMus%E2%80%93Museum_of_Modern_Art%E2%80%93Costakis_Collection) and two theatres of the [National Theatre of Northern Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Theatre_of_Northern_Greece)), former Catholic Lazarist Monastery (Moni Lazariston)

Other districts of the wider urban area of Thessaloniki are Ampelokipi, Eleftherio – Kordelio, Menemeni, Evosmos, Ilioupoli, Stavroupoli, Nikopoli, Neapoli, Polichni, Paeglos, Meteora, Agios Pavlos, Kalamaria, Pylaia and the Sykies. Northwestern Thessaloniki is home to *Moni Lazariston*, located in [Stavroupoli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stavroupoli), which today forms one of the most important cultural centres for the city, including [MOMus–Museum of Modern Art–Costakis Collection](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MOMus%E2%80%93Museum_of_Modern_Art%E2%80%93Costakis_Collection) and two theatres of the [National Theatre of Northern Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Theatre_of_Northern_Greece).[^cite_note-211][^cite_note-212]

In northwestern Thessaloniki many cultural premises exist, such as the open-air Theater *Manos Katrakis* in Sykies, the Museum of Refugee Hellenism in Neapolis, the municipal theatre and the open-air theatre in Neapoli and the New Cultural Centre of Menemeni (Ellis Alexiou Street).[^cite_note-213] The Stavroupolis Botanical Garden on Perikleous Street includes 1,000 species of plants and is a 5-acre (2.0 ha) oasis of greenery. The Environmental Education Centre in Kordelio was designed in 1997 and is one of a few public buildings of bioclimatic design in Thessaloniki.[^cite_note-214]

Northwest Thessaloniki forms the main entry point into the city of Thessaloniki with the avenues of Monastiriou, [Miki Theodoraki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikis_Theodorakis_Avenue) and 26is Octovriou passing through it, as well as the extension of the A1 motorway, feeding into Thessaloniki's city centre. The area is home to the [Macedonia InterCity Bus Terminal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thessaloniki_Bus_Station) ([KTEL](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/KTEL_\(Greece\))), the [Thessaloniki railway station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thessaloniki_railway_station), and the [Zeitenlik](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zeitenlik) [Allied](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allies_\(World_War_I\)) memorial [military cemetery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_cemetery).

Monuments have also been erected in honour of the fighters of the [Greek Resistance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Resistance), as in these areas the Resistance was very active: the monument of Greek National Resistance in Sykies, the monument of Greek National Resistance in Stavroupolis, the Statue of the struggling Mother in Eptalofos Square and the monument of the young Greeks who were executed by the Nazis on 11 May 1944 in Xirokrini. In Eptalofos, on 15 May 1941, one month after the occupation of the country, the first resistance organization in Greece, "Eleftheria", was founded, with its newspaper and the first illegal printing house in the city of Thessaloniki.[^cite_note-215][^cite_note-καζάνας1-216]

Today southeastern Thessaloniki has in some way become an extension of the city centre, with the avenues of Megalou Alexandrou, Georgiou Papandreou (Antheon), Vasileos Georgiou, Vasilissis Olgas, Delfon, Konstantinou Karamanli (Nea Egnatia) and Papanastasiou passing through it, enclosing an area traditionally called Ντεπώ (*Depó*_,_ lit. Dépôt), from the name of the old tram station, owned by a French company.

The municipality of [Kalamaria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kalamaria) is also located in southeastern Thessaloniki and was firstly, inhabited mainly by Greek refugees from [Asia Minor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asia_Minor) and [East Thrace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Thrace) after 1922.[^cite_note-217] There are built the [Northern Greece Naval Command](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Greece_Naval_Command) and the old royal palace (called [Palataki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palataki_\(Thessaloniki\))), located on the most westerly point of [Mikro Emvolo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikro_Emvolo) cape.

### Paleochristian and Byzantine monuments (UNESCO)

Main article: [Paleochristian and Byzantine monuments of Thessaloniki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paleochristian_and_Byzantine_monuments_of_Thessaloniki)

See also: [Byzantine architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_architecture)



The [church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hagios_Demetrios) of [Saint Demetrius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_Demetrius), patron saint of the city, built in the fourth century, is the largest basilica in Greece and one of the city's most prominent Paleochristian monuments.



[Hagia Sophia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hagia_Sophia,_Thessaloniki), Thessaloniki



[Panagia Chalkeon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Panagia_Chalkeon) church in Thessaloniki (1028 AD), one of the 15 UNESCO World Heritage Sites in the city



[The Byzantine Bath of the Upper Town](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_Bath_\(Thessaloniki\))

Because of Thessaloniki's importance during the [early Christian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_Christianity) and [Byzantine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_Empire) periods, the city is host to several Paleochristian monuments that have significantly contributed to the development of [Byzantine art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_art) and architecture throughout the Byzantine Empire as well as [Serbia](29265.md).[^cite_note-UNESCO-165] The evolution of Imperial [Byzantine architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_architecture) and the prosperity of Thessaloniki go hand in hand, especially during the first years of the Empire,[^cite_note-UNESCO-165] when the city continued to flourish. It was at that time that the [Complex of](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arch_of_Galerius_and_Rotunda) [Roman emperor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_emperor) [Galerius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galerius) was built, as well as the first church of [Hagios Demetrios](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_Demetrius).[^cite_note-UNESCO-165]

By the eighth century, the city had become an important administrative centre of the [Byzantine Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_Empire), and handled much of the Empire's [Balkan](4829.md) affairs.[^cite_note-AUTH_2-218] During that time, the city saw the creation of more notable Christian churches that are now part of Thessaloniki's UNESCO World Heritage Site, such as the [Church of Saint Catherine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_of_Saint_Catherine,_Thessaloniki), the [Hagia Sophia of Thessaloniki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hagia_Sophia_\(Thessaloniki\)), the [Church of the Acheiropoietos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_of_the_Acheiropoietos), the [Church of Panagia Chalkeon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_of_Panagia_Chalkeon).[^cite_note-UNESCO-165] When the [Ottoman Empire](22278.md) took control of Thessaloniki in 1430, most of the city's churches were converted into [mosques](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mosques),[^cite_note-UNESCO-165] but have survived to this day. Travellers such as [Paul Lucas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Lucas_\(traveller\)) and [Abdulmejid I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdulmejid_I)[^cite_note-UNESCO-165] document the city's wealth in Christian monuments during the years of Ottoman control of the city.

The church of [Hagios Demetrios](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hagios_Demetrios) burned down during the [Great Thessaloniki Fire of 1917](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Thessaloniki_Fire_of_1917), as did many other city monuments, but it was rebuilt. During [World War II](32927.md), the city was extensively bombed and as such many of Thessaloniki's Paleochristian and Byzantine monuments were heavily damaged.[^cite_note-AUTH_2-218] Some of the sites were not restored until the 1980s. Thessaloniki has more monuments listed as a UNESCO World Heritage Site than any other city in Greece, a total of 15 monuments.[^cite_note-UNESCO-165] They have been listed since 1988.[^cite_note-UNESCO-165]

### Urban sculpture



The equestrian statue of [Alexander the Great](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_the_Great) on the promenade

There are around 150 statues or busts in the city.[^cite_note-219] Probably the most famous one is the equestrian statue of [Alexander the Great](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_the_Great) on the promenade, placed in 1973 and created by sculptor Evangelos Moustakas. An equestrian statue of [Constantine I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constantine_I_of_Greece), by sculptor [Georgios Dimitriades](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgios_Dimitriades?action=edit\&redlink=1), is located in Demokratias Square. Other notable statues include that of [Eleftherios Venizelos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eleftherios_Venizelos) by sculptor [Giannis Pappas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Giannis_Pappas?action=edit\&redlink=1), [Pavlos Melas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pavlos_Melas) by [Natalia Mela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natalia_Mela), the statue of [Emmanouel Pappas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emmanouel_Pappas) by [Memos Makris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Memos_Makris), [Chrysostomos of Smyrna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chrysostomos_of_Smyrna) by [Athanasios Apartis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Athanase_Apartis), [Aristotle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aristotle) on [Aristotelous Square](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aristotelous_Square) and such as various creations by [George Zongolopoulos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Zongolopoulos), [Philip II of Macedon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philip_II_of_Macedon) opposite the White Tower.

### Thessaloniki 2012 Programme



Aerial view of the newest section of the promenade (Nea Paralia), which was opened to the public in January 2014

With the 100th anniversary of the 1912 incorporation of Thessaloniki into Greece, the government announced a large-scale redevelopment programme for the city of Thessaloniki, which aims in addressing the current environmental and spatial problems[^cite_note-2012-1-220] that the city faces. More specifically, the programme will drastically change the physiognomy of the city[^cite_note-2012-1-220] by relocating the Thessaloniki International Exhibition Centre and grounds of the [Thessaloniki International Fair](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thessaloniki_International_Fair) outside the city centre and turning the current location into a large metropolitan park,[^cite_note-2012-2-221] redeveloping the coastal front of the city,[^cite_note-2012-2-221] relocating the city's numerous military camps and using the grounds and facilities to create large parklands and cultural centres;[^cite_note-2012-2-221] and the complete redevelopment of the harbour and the *Lachanokipoi* and *Dendropotamos* districts (behind and near the [Port of Thessaloniki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Thessaloniki)) into a commercial business district,[^cite_note-2012-2-221] with possible [highrise](29485.md) developments.[^cite_note-222]

The plan also envisions the creation of new wide avenues in the outskirts of the city[^cite_note-2012-2-221] and the creation of pedestrian-only zones in the city centre.[^cite_note-2012-2-221] Furthermore, the program includes plans to expand the jurisdiction of *Seich Sou* Forest National Park[^cite_note-2012-1-220] and the improvement of accessibility to and from the Old Town.[^cite_note-2012-1-220] The ministry has said that the project will take an estimated 15 years to be completed, in 2025.[^cite_note-2012-2-221]

Part of the plan has been implemented with extensive pedestrianisations within the city centre by the municipality of Thessaloniki and the revitalisation the eastern urban waterfront/promenade, Νέα Παραλία (*Néa Paralía*, lit. new promenade), with a modern and vibrant design. Its first section opened in 2008, having been awarded as the best public project in Greece of the last five years by the Hellenic Institute of Architecture.[^cite_note-223]

The municipality of Thessaloniki's budget for the reconstruction of important areas of the city and the completion of the waterfront, opened in January 2014, was estimated at [€](9472.md)28.2 million ([US\$](18717338.md)39.9 million) for the year 2011 alone.[^cite_note-224]

See also: [Category\:Companies based in Thessaloniki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Category\:Companies_based_in_Thessaloniki) and [Economy of Greece](12113.md)

The old building of [Banque de Salonique](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banque_de_Salonique), now Stoa Malakopi



A building of the [Bank of Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bank_of_Greece)

Thessaloniki rose to economic prominence as a major economic hub in the [Balkans](4829.md) during the years of the [Roman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Empire). The [Pax Romana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pax_Romana) and the city's strategic position allowed for the facilitation of trade between [Rome](25458.md) and [Byzantium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantium) (later [Constantinople](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constantinople) and now [Istanbul](3391396.md)) through Thessaloniki by means of the [Via Egnatia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Via_Egnatia).[^cite_note-Companion_to_Macedonia-228] The Via Egnatia also functioned as an important line of communication between the Roman Empire and the nations of Asia,[^cite_note-Companion_to_Macedonia-228] particularly in relation to the [Silk Road](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Silk_Road).

With the partition of the Roman Empire into [East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_Empire) (Byzantine) and [West](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Roman_Empire), Thessaloniki became the second-largest city of the Eastern Roman Empire after New Rome (Constantinople) in terms of economic might.[^cite_note-DCCXVI-52][^cite_note-Companion_to_Macedonia-228] Under the Empire, Thessaloniki was the largest port in the Balkans.[^cite_note-Economic_Ideas-229] As the city passed from Byzantium to the [Republic of Venice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_of_Venice) in 1423, it was subsequently conquered by the [Ottoman Empire](22278.md). Under Ottoman rule the city retained its position as the most important trading hub in the Balkans.[^cite_note-Economic_History-96] Manufacturing, shipping and trade were the most important components of the city's economy during the Ottoman period,[^cite_note-Economic_History-96] and the majority of the city's trade at the time was controlled by ethnic [Greeks](42056.md).[^cite_note-Economic_History-96] Plus, the [Jewish community](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jews_of_Thessaloniki) was also an important factor in the trade sector.

Historically important industries for the economy of Thessaloniki included [tobacco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tobacco_industry) (in 1946 35% of all tobacco companies in Greece were headquartered in the city, and 44% in 1979)[^cite_note-Tobacco-230] and [banking](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bank) (in Ottoman years Thessaloniki was a major centre for investment from western Europe, with the [Banque de Salonique](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banque_de_Salonique) having a capital of 20 million [French francs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_franc) in 1909).[^cite_note-Economic_History-96]

### Services

See also: [Port of Thessaloniki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Thessaloniki)



The port in 2009

The [service sector](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secondary_sector_of_the_economy) accounts for nearly two-thirds of the total labour force of Thessaloniki.[^cite_note-Globalization-231] Of those working in services, 20% were employed in trade; 13% in education and healthcare; 7.1% in real estate; 6.3% in transport, communications and storage; 6.1% in the finance industry and service-providing organizations; 5.7% in public administration and insurance services; and 5.4% in hotels and restaurants.[^cite_note-Globalization-231]

The city's port, the [Port of Thessaloniki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Thessaloniki), is one of the largest ports in the Aegean and as a [free port](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_port), it functions as a major gateway to the Balkan hinterland.[^cite_note-Development-11][^cite_note-THPA-232] In 2010, more than 15.8 million tons of products went through the city's port,[^cite_note-Eurostat_ports-233] making it the second-largest port in Greece after [Aghioi Theodoroi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aghioi_Theodoroi), surpassing [Piraeus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Piraeus). At 273,282 [TEUs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twenty-foot_equivalent_unit), it is also Greece's second-largest container port after Piraeus.[^cite_note-Statistical_Data-234] As a result, the city is a major transportation hub for the whole of south-eastern Europe,[^cite_note-235] carrying, among other things, trade to and from the neighbouring countries.

In recent years Thessaloniki has begun to turn into a major port for [cruising](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cruising_\(maritime\)) in the eastern [Mediterranean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mediterranean).[^cite_note-THPA-232] The Greek ministry of tourism considers Thessaloniki to be Greece's second most important commercial port,[^cite_note-Royal_Caribbean-236] and companies such as [Royal Caribbean International](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Caribbean_International) have expressed interest in adding the Port of Thessaloniki to their destinations.[^cite_note-Royal_Caribbean-236] A total of 30 cruise ships are expected to arrive at Thessaloniki in 2011.[^cite_note-Royal_Caribbean-236]



The GDP of Thessaloniki in comparison to that of Attica and the rest of the country (2012)

### Companies

#### Recent history

After [WWII](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WWII) and the [Greek Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Civil_War), heavy industrialization of the city's suburbs began in the mid-1950s.[^cite_note-237]

During the 1980s, a spate of factory shutdowns occurred, mostly of automobile manufacturers, such as [Agricola](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agricola_\(vehicles\)), [AutoDiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AutoDiana), [EBIAM](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/EBIAM), [Motoemil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Motoemil), [Pantelemidis-TITAN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pantelemidis-TITAN) and [C.AR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/C.AR_\(automobiles\)). Since the 1990s, companies took advantage of cheaper labour markets and more lax regulations than in other countries, and among the largest companies to shut down factories were [Goodyear](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Goodyear_Tire_and_Rubber_Company),[^cite_note-238] AVEZ pasta industry (one of the first industrial factories in northern Greece, built in 1926),[^cite_note-239] [Philkeram Johnson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philkeram_Johnson), AGNO dairy and VIAMIL.

However, Thessaloniki still remains a major business hub in the Balkans and Greece, with a number of important Greek companies headquartered in the city, such as the [Hellenic Vehicle Industry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hellenic_Vehicle_Industry) (ELVO), [Namco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Namco_\(automobiles\)), [Astra Airlines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Astra_Airlines), [Ellinair](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ellinair), [Pyramis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pyramis) and [MLS Multimedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MLS_Multimedia), which introduced the first Greek-built [smartphone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Smartphone) in 2012.[^cite_note-240]

#### Industry

In early 1960s, with the collaboration of [Standard Oil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Standard_Oil) and [ESSO-Pappas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ESSO-Pappas?action=edit\&redlink=1), a large industrial zone was created, containing refineries, [oil refinery](195137.md) and [steel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steel) production (owned by [Hellenic Steel Co.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hellenic_Steel_Co.?action=edit\&redlink=1)). The zone attracted also a series of different factories during the next decades.

[Titan Cement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titan_Cement) has also facilities outside the city, on the road to [Serres](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serres),[^cite_note-241] such as the [AGET Heracles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heracles_General_Cement), a member of the [Lafarge](41902084.md) group, and [Alumil SA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alumil_Aluminium_Industry_S.A.).

Multinational companies such as [Air Liquide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Air_Liquide), [Cyanamid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Cyanamid), [Nestlé](160227.md), [Pfizer](62304.md), [Coca-Cola Hellenic Bottling Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coca-Cola_Hellenic_Bottling_Company) and [Vivartia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vivartia) have also industrial facilities in the suburbs of the city.[^cite_note-242]

#### Foodstuff

Foodstuff or drink companies headquartered in the city include the [Macedonian Milk Industry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mevgal) (Mevgal), [Allatini](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allatini_\(company\)), [Barbastathis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barbastathis), [Hellenic Sugar Industry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hellenic_Sugar_Industry), [Haitoglou Bros](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haitoglou_Bros), [Mythos Brewery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mythos_Brewery), [Malamatina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malamatina), while the [Goody's](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Goody's_Burger_House_\(restaurant\)) chain started from the city.

The [American Farm School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Farm_School) also is an important contributor to local food production.[^cite_note-243]

### Macroeconomic indicators

In 2011, the [regional unit of Thessaloniki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thessaloniki_\(regional_unit\)) had a [Gross Domestic Product](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gross_Domestic_Product) of [€](9472.md)18.293 billion (ranked second amongst the country's regional units),[^cite_note-Eurostat_GDPs-244] comparable to [Bahrain](18933277.md) or [Cyprus](5593.md), and a per capita of €15,900 (ranked 16th).[^cite_note-Eurostat_GDPs-244] In [Purchasing Power Parity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Purchasing_Power_Parity) (PPP), the same indicators are €19,851 billion (2nd)[^cite_note-Eurostat_GDPs-244] and €17,200 (15th) respectively.[^cite_note-Eurostat_GDPs-244] In terms of comparison with the [European Union](9317.md) average, Thessaloniki's GDP per capita indicator stands at 63% the EU average[^cite_note-Eurostat_GDPs-244] and 69% in PPP[^cite_note-Eurostat_GDPs-244] – this is comparable to the German state of [Brandenburg](3765.md).[^cite_note-Eurostat_GDPs-244] Overall, Thessaloniki accounts for 8.9% of the total [economy of Greece](12113.md).[^cite_note-Eurostat_GDPs-244] Between 1995 and 2008 Thessaloniki's GDP saw an average growth rate of 4.1% per annum (ranging from +14.5% in 1996 to −11.1% in 2005) while in 2011 the economy contracted by −7.8%.[^cite_note-Eurostat_GDPs-244]

## Demographics

### Historical ethnic statistics

The tables below show the ethnic statistics of Thessaloniki during the end of the 19th and the beginning of the 20th century.

The municipality of Thessaloniki is the most populous in the Thessaloniki Urban Area. Its population has increased in the latest census and the metropolitan area's population rose to over one million. The city forms the base of the [Thessaloniki metropolitan area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thessaloniki_metropolitan_area), with latest census in 2021 giving it a population of 1,091,424.[^cite_note-2011census-246]

### Jews of Thessaloniki

Main article: [History of the Jews of Thessaloniki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Jews_in_Thessaloniki)



Paths of Jewish immigration to the city

| icon | This article **needs [more citations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia\:Verifiability)**. Please help [improve this article](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:EditPage/Thessaloniki) by [adding citations to reliable sources](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:Referencing_for_beginners). Unsourced material may be challenged and [removed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia\:Verifiability#Burden_of_evidence). *Find sources:* ["Thessaloniki"](https://www.google.com/search?as_eq=wikipedia\&q=%22Thessaloniki%22) – [news](https://www.google.com/search?tbm=nws\&q=%22Thessaloniki%22+-wikipedia\&tbs=ar\:1) **·** [newspapers](https://www.google.com/search?\&q=%22Thessaloniki%22\&tbs=bkt\:s\&tbm=bks) **·** [books](https://www.google.com/search?tbs=bks\:1\&q=%22Thessaloniki%22+-wikipedia) **·** [scholar](https://scholar.google.com/scholar?q=%22Thessaloniki%22) **·** [JSTOR](https://www.jstor.org/action/doBasicSearch?Query=%22Thessaloniki%22\&acc=on\&wc=on) *(February 2019)*_&#x20;([Learn how and when to remove this message](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:Maintenance_template_removal))_ |
| --- | --- |

The Jewish population in Greece is the oldest in mainland Europe (see [Romaniotes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romaniote_Jews)). When [Paul the Apostle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_the_Apostle) came to Thessaloniki, he taught in the area of what today is called *Upper City*. Later, during the Ottoman period, with the coming of Sephardic Jews from Spain, the community of Thessaloniki became mostly Sephardic. Thessaloniki became the largest centre in Europe of the [Sephardic Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sephardic_Jews), who nicknamed the city *la madre de Israel* (Israel's mother)[^cite_note-Holocaust-152] and "Jerusalem of the Balkans".[^cite_note-251] It also included the historically significant and ancient Greek-speaking Romaniote community. During the [Ottoman](22278.md) era, Thessaloniki's Sephardic community was half of the population according to the Ottoman Census of 1902 and almost 40% the city's population of 157,000 about 1913; Jewish merchants were prominent in commerce until the ethnic Greek population increased after Thessaloniki was incorporated into the Kingdom of Greece in 1913. By the 1680s, about 300 families of Sephardic Jews, followers of [Sabbatai Zevi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sabbatai_Zevi), had converted to [Islam](6037917.md), becoming a sect known as the *[Dönmeh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/D%C3%B6nmeh)* (convert), and migrated to Salonika, whose population was majority Jewish. They established an active community that thrived for about 250 years. Many of their descendants later became prominent in trade.[^cite_note-252] Many Jewish inhabitants of Thessaloniki spoke [Judeo-Spanish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judaeo-Spanish), the [Romance language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romance_languages) of the Sephardic Jews.[^cite_note-253]



Jewish family of Salonika in 1917

From the second half of the 19th century with the Ottoman reforms, the Jewish community had a new revival. Many French and especially [Italian Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italian_Jews) (from [Livorno](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Livorno) and other cities), influential in introducing new methods of education and developing new schools and intellectual environments for the Jewish population, were established in Thessaloniki. Such modernists introduced also new techniques and ideas from the industrialised Western Europe and from the 1880s the city began to industrialize. The Italian-Jewish Allatini brothers led Jewish entrepreneurship, establishing [milling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mill_\(grinding\)) and other food industries, [brickmaking](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brick) and processing plants for [tobacco](30942.md). Several traders supported the introduction of a large textile-production industry, superseding the weaving of cloth in a system of artisanal production. Notable names of the era include among others the Italo-Jewish Modiano family and the [Allatini](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allatini_\(company\)). [Benrubis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benrubi_SA) founded also in 1880 one of the first retail companies in the Balkans.

After the [Balkan Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balkan_Wars), Thessaloniki was incorporated into the [Kingdom of Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Greece) in 1913. At first, the community feared that the annexation would lead to difficulties and during the first years its political stance was, in general, anti-[Venizelist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Venizelism) and pro-royalist/conservative. The [Great Thessaloniki Fire of 1917](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Thessaloniki_Fire_of_1917) during [World War I](4764461.md) burned much of the centre of the city and left 50,000 Jews homeless of the total of 72,000 residents who were burned out.[^cite_note-History-142] Having lost homes and their businesses, many Jews emigrated to the United States, Palestine, and Paris. They could not wait for the government to create a new urban plan for rebuilding, which was eventually done.[^cite_note-254]

After the [Greco-Turkish War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greco-Turkish_War_\(1919%E2%80%931922\)) in 1922 and the bilateral population exchange between Greece and Turkey, many refugees came to Greece. Nearly 100,000 ethnic Greeks resettled in Thessaloniki, reducing the proportion of Jews in the total community. After this, Jews made up about 20% of the city's population. During the interwar period, Greece granted Jewish citizens the same [civil rights](37071.md) as other Greek citizens.[^cite_note-History-142] In March 1926, Greece re-emphasized that all citizens of Greece enjoyed equal rights, and a considerable proportion of the city's Jews decided to stay. During the [Metaxas regime](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metaxas_regime), the stance towards Jews improved even more.



"Jews not welcomed" sign during the [Axis occupation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Axis_occupation_of_Greece_during_World_War_II)



[Monastir Synagogue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monastir_Synagogue_\(Thessaloniki\))

[World War II](32927.md) brought disaster for the Jewish Greeks, since in 1941 the Germans occupied Greece and began actions against the Jewish population. Greeks of the [Resistance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_resistance) helped save some of the Jewish residents.[^cite_note-Holocaust-152] By the 1940s, the great majority of the Jewish Greek community firmly identified as both Greek and Jewish. According to [Misha Glenny](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Misha_Glenny), such Greek Jews had largely not encountered "anti-Semitism as in its North European form."[^cite_note-255]

In 1943, the Nazis began brutal actions against the historic Jewish population in Thessaloniki, forcing them into a [ghetto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghetto) near the railroad lines and beginning [deportation](412990.md) to concentration and labor camps. They deported and exterminated approximately 94% of Thessaloniki's remaining Jews of all ages during [the Holocaust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Holocaust).[^cite_note-Holocaust2-256] In addition to the extermination of the Jewish community of the city, the Nazis systematically razed Jewish monuments and history. Prominently, the destruction included the bulldozing of the ancient [Jewish Cemetery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_cemetery_of_Salonica), which at that point included around 500,000 tombs, with the marble gravestones being repurposed as building material for roads and new construction. The Thessaloniki Holocaust memorial in Eleftherias ("Freedom") Square was built in 1997 in memory of all the Jewish people from Thessaloniki murdered in the Holocaust. The site was chosen because it was the place where Jewish residents were rounded up before embarking on trains for concentration camps.[^cite_note-257][^cite_note-258] Today, a community of around 1,200 remains in the city.[^cite_note-Holocaust-152] Communities of descendants of Thessaloniki Jews – both Sephardic and Romaniote – live in other areas, mainly the United States and Israel.[^cite_note-Holocaust2-256] Israeli singer [Yehuda Poliker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yehuda_Poliker) recorded a song about the Jewish people of Thessaloniki, called "Wait for me, Thessaloniki".

On 15 March, 2026, 83 years after the first deportation to [Auschwitz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Auschwitz_concentration_camp), hundreds of people march in the streets under the sign saying: "[Never Again](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Never_again)".[^cite_note-\:1-259] The first train departed from Thessaloniki on 15, March 1943, followed by many more, eventually taking approximately 50,000 people to the Auschwitz-Birkenau camp for extermination.[^cite_note-\:1-259]

### Others

Unsourced material may be challenged and [removed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia\:Verifiability#Burden_of_evidence). *Find sources:* ["Thessaloniki"](https://www.google.com/search?as_eq=wikipedia\&q=%22Thessaloniki%22) – [news](https://www.google.com/search?tbm=nws\&q=%22Thessaloniki%22+-wikipedia\&tbs=ar\:1) **·** [newspapers](https://www.google.com/search?\&q=%22Thessaloniki%22\&tbs=bkt\:s\&tbm=bks) **·** [books](https://www.google.com/search?tbs=bks\:1\&q=%22Thessaloniki%22+-wikipedia) **·** [scholar](https://scholar.google.com/scholar?q=%22Thessaloniki%22) **·** [JSTOR](https://www.jstor.org/action/doBasicSearch?Query=%22Thessaloniki%22\&acc=on\&wc=on) *(February 2019)*_&#x20;([Learn how and when to remove this message](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:Maintenance_template_removal))_ |
| --- | --- |

Since the late 19th century, many merchants from Western Europe (mainly from France and Italy) were established in the city. They had an important role in the social and economic life of the city and introduced new industrial techniques. Their main district was what is known today as the "Frankish district" /"Φραγκομαχαλάς" (near [Ladadika](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ladadika)), where the Catholic church designed by [Vitaliano Poselli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vitaliano_Poselli) is also situated.[^cite_note-261][^cite_note-262] A part of them left after the incorporation of the city into the Greek kingdom, while others, who were of Jewish faith, were exterminated by the [Nazis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nazis).

The [Bulgarian](151876.md) community of the city increased during the late 19th century.[^cite_note-263] The community had a Men's High School, a Girl's High School, a trade union and a gymnastics society. A large part of them were Catholics, as a result of actions by the [Lazarists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congregation_of_the_Mission) society, which had its base in the city.

Another group is the [Armenian](387816.md) community which dates back to the Byzantine and Ottoman periods. During the 20th century, after the [Armenian genocide](140376.md) and the defeat of the Greek army in the [Greco-Turkish War (1919–22)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greco-Turkish_War_\(1919%E2%80%9322\)), many fled to Greece including Thessaloniki. There is also an Armenian cemetery and an Armenian church at the centre of the city.[^cite_note-264]

### Leisure and entertainment



The building of the *Society of Macedonian studies*, seat of the [National Theatre of Northern Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Theatre_of_Northern_Greece)

Thessaloniki is regarded not only as the cultural and entertainment capital of northern Greece[^cite_note-AUTH_2-218][^cite_note-Lonely_Planet_guide-265] but also the cultural capital of the country as a whole.[^cite_note-Lonely_Planet_Thessaloniki-12] The city's main theaters, run by the [National Theatre of Northern Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Theatre_of_Northern_Greece) ([Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_language): Κρατικό Θέατρο Βορείου Ελλάδος) which was established in 1961,[^cite_note-NTNG-266] include the *Theater of the Society of Macedonian Studies*, where the National Theater is based, the *Royal Theater* (Βασιλικό Θέατρο) – the first base of the National Theater – *Moni Lazariston*, and the *Earth Theater* and *Forest Theater*, both amphitheatrical open-air theatres overlooking the city.[^cite_note-NTNG-266]



[Thessaloniki Concert Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thessaloniki_Concert_Hall)

The title of the [European Capital of Culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Capital_of_Culture) in 1997 saw the birth of the city's first opera[^cite_note-267] and today forms an independent section of the [National Theatre of Northern Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Theatre_of_Northern_Greece).[^cite_note-268] The opera is based at the [Thessaloniki Concert Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thessaloniki_Concert_Hall), one of the largest concert halls in Greece. Recently a second building was also constructed and designed by Japanese architect [Arata Isozaki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arata_Isozaki). Thessaloniki is also the seat of two symphony orchestras, the *[Thessaloniki State Orchestra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thessaloniki_State_Orchestra?action=edit\&redlink=1)&#x20;*&#x20;and the *Symphony Orchestra of the Municipality of Thessaloniki*. *Olympion Theater*, the site of the [Thessaloniki International Film Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thessaloniki_International_Film_Festival) and the *Plateia Assos Odeon multiplex* are the two major cinemas in downtown Thessaloniki. The city also has a number of multiplex cinemas in major shopping malls in the suburbs, most notably in [Mediterranean Cosmos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mediterranean_Cosmos), the largest retail and entertainment development in the Balkans.

[Tsimiski Avenue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tsimiski_Street), Mitropoleos Ave and Proxenou Koromila Street are the city's most famous shopping streets and are among Greece's most expensive and exclusive high streets. The city is also home to one of Greece's most famous and prestigious hotels, [Makedonia Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Makedonia_Palace) 5-star hotel, the Hyatt Regency Casino Thessaloniki and hotel (the biggest casino in Greece and one of the biggest in Europe) and Waterland, the largest water park in southeastern Europe.

The city has long been known in Greece for its vibrant city culture, including having the most cafes and bars per capita of any city in Europe; and as having some of the best nightlife and entertainment in the country, thanks to its large young population and multicultural feel. [Lonely Planet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lonely_Planet) listed Thessaloniki among the world's "ultimate party cities".[^cite_note-269] Restaurant and bar areas include the [Ladadika](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ladadika) area near the port, Nikis Avenue on the waterfront, the alleyways between [Agias Sofias Square](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agias_Sofias_Square) and Aristotelous Square, the waterfront of [Kalamarià](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kalamaria) neighbourhood, and the [Eptapirgio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heptapyrgion_\(Thessaloniki\)) neighbourhood overlooking the city.

### Parks and recreation



Marina of [Aretsou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nea_Aretsou)



Part of the coastline of the southeastern suburb of [Peraia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peraia,_Thessaloniki) on the [Thermaic Gulf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thermaic_Gulf), with views towards Thessaloniki

Although Thessaloniki is not renowned for its parks and greenery throughout its urban area, where green spaces are few, it has several large open spaces around its waterfront, namely the central city gardens of *Palios Zoologikos Kipos* (which is recently being redeveloped to also include rock climbing facilities, a new skatepark and paintball range),[^cite_note-270] the park of *Pedion tou Areos*, which also holds the city's annual floral expo; and the parks of the Renowned *Nea Paralia* (Restructured waterfront) spanning for 3 km (2 mi) along the coast, beginning from the [White Tower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_Tower_of_Thessaloniki) to the [concert hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thessaloniki_Concert_Hall).

The *Nea Paralia* parks are used throughout the year for a variety of events, while they open up to the Thessaloniki waterfront, which is lined with several cafés and bars; and during summer is full of Thessalonians enjoying their long evening walks (referred to as *"volta"* or *“kafès”* and is embedded into the culture of the city). Having undergone an extensive revitalization, the city's waterfront today features a total of 12 thematic gardens/parks.[^cite_note-271]

Thessaloniki's proximity to places such as the national parks of [Pieria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pieria_\(regional_unit\)) and beaches of [Chalkidiki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chalkidiki) allow its residents to easily have access to some of the best outdoor recreation in Europe; however, the city is also right next to the *[Séih Su](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seih_Sou)* national park, just 3.5 km (2 mi) away from Thessaloniki's city centre; and offers residents and visitors alike, quiet viewpoints towards the city, mountain bike trails and landscaped hiking paths.[^cite_note-272] The city's [zoo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zoo), which is operated by the municipality of Thessaloniki, is also located nearby the national park.[^cite_note-273]

Other recreation spaces throughout the [Thessaloniki metropolitan area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thessaloniki_metropolitan_area) include the *Fragma Thermis*, a landscaped parkland near [Thermi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thermi) and the Delta wetlands west of the city centre; while urban beaches of Peréa, Nea Mihaniona and Ayia Triada, continuously awarded with the [Blue Flag beach](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blue_Flag_beach) award,[^cite_note-274] are located along the 10 km (6 mi) coastline of Thessaloniki's southeastern suburbs of [Thermaikos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thermaikos) gulf, about 20 km (12 mi) away from the city centre.

### Museums and galleries

Main article: [List of museums in Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_museums_in_Greece)



View of the [Museum of Byzantine Culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Museum_of_Byzantine_Culture)



The Museum of Byzantine Culture, Thessaloniki

Because of the city's rich and diverse history, Thessaloniki houses many museums dealing with many different eras in history. Two of the city's most famous museums include the [National Archaeological Museum of Thessaloniki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Archaeological_Museum_of_Thessaloniki) and the [Byzantine Culture Museum of Thessaloniki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Museum_of_Byzantine_Culture).



Head of Isis, Archaeological Museum, Thessaloniki. Early 3rd century BC (2021).

The [Archaeological Museum of Thessaloniki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Archaeological_Museum_of_Thessaloniki), situated in Manolis Andronikos Street–ad honorem of the famous archaeologist that excavated Royal Palaces of the Macedonian Kingdom specifically King Phillip II's tomb, was established in 1962 and houses some of the most important [ancient Macedonian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Macedon) artifacts,[^cite_note-275] including an extensive collection of golden artwork from the royal palaces of [Aigai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aegae_\(Macedon\)) and [Pella](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pella).[^cite_note-276] It also houses exhibits from Macedon's prehistoric past, dating from the [Neolithic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neolithic_age) to the [Bronze Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bronze_Age).[^cite_note-277]

The [Museum of Byzantine Culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Museum_of_Byzantine_Culture) is one of the city's most famous museums, showcasing the city's glorious [Byzantine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_Empire) past.[^cite_note-278] The museum was also awarded [Council of Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_Europe)'s museum prize in 2005.[^cite_note-279] The museum of the [White Tower of Thessaloniki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_Tower_of_Thessaloniki) houses a series of galleries relating to the city's past, from the creation of the White Tower until recent years.[^cite_note-280]

One of the most modern museums in the city is the [Thessaloniki Science Center and Technology Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thessaloniki_Science_Center_and_Technology_Museum) (*NOESIS*) and is one of the most high-tech museums in Greece and southeastern Europe.[^cite_note-NOESIS-281] It features the largest [planetarium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planetarium) in Greece, a cosmotheatre with the country's largest [flat screen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flat_screen), an [amphitheater](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amphitheater), a [motion simulator](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Motion_simulator) with 3D projection and 6-axis movement and exhibition spaces.[^cite_note-NOESIS-281] Other industrial and technological museums in the city include the [Railway Museum of Thessaloniki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Railway_Museum_of_Thessaloniki), which houses an original [Orient Express](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orient_Express) train, the [War Museum of Thessaloniki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_Museum_of_Thessaloniki) and others. The city also has a number of educational and sports museums, including the [Thessaloniki History Centre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thessaloniki_History_Centre) and the [Thessaloniki Olympic Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thessaloniki_Olympic_Museum).

The [Atatürk Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atat%C3%BCrk_Museum_\(Thessaloniki\)) in Thessaloniki is the historic house where [Mustafa Kemal Atatürk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mustafa_Kemal_Atat%C3%BCrk), founder of modern-day Turkey, was born. The house is now part of the Turkish consulate complex, but admission to the museum is free.[^cite_note-Ataturk-282] The museum contains historic information about Mustafa Kemal Atatürk and his life, especially while he was in Thessaloniki.[^cite_note-Ataturk-282] Other ethnological museums of the sort include the [Historical Museum of the Balkan Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Historical_Museum_of_the_Balkan_Wars), the [Jewish Museum of Thessaloniki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_Museum_of_Thessaloniki) and the [Museum of the Macedonian Struggle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Museum_for_the_Macedonian_Struggle_\(Thessaloniki\)), containing information about the anti-Ottoman rebellions of the late 19th and early 20th centuries.[^cite_note-283] Construction on the [Holocaust Museum of Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holocaust_Museum_of_Greece) began in the city in 2018.[^cite_note-efsyn.gr-208] It is set to open in 2026.[^cite_note-284]

The city also has a number of important art galleries. Such include the [Macedonian Museum of Contemporary Art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Macedonian_Museum_of_Contemporary_Art), housing exhibitions from a number of well-known [Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_people) and foreign artists.[^cite_note-285] The [Teloglion Foundation of Art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teloglion_Foundation_of_Art) is part of [Aristotle University of Thessaloniki](715160.md) and includes an extensive collection of works by important artists of the 19th and 20th centuries, including works by prominent Greeks and native Thessalonians.[^cite_note-286] The [Thessaloniki Museum of Photography](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thessaloniki_Museum_of_Photography) also houses a number of important exhibitions, and is located within the old port of Thessaloniki.[^cite_note-287]

### Archaeological sites

See also: [Thessaloniki Metro § Archaeology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thessaloniki_Metro#Archaeology)

The Roman Forum (Ancient Agora)

View of the [Roman Forum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Forum_\(Thessaloniki\)) (Ancient Agora)

Thessaloniki is home to a number of prominent archaeological sites. Apart from its recognized UNESCO World Heritage Sites, Thessaloniki features a large two-terraced [Roman forum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Forum_\(Thessaloniki\))[^cite_note-Frommer's_Greece_Forum_1-288] featuring two-storey [stoas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stoa),[^cite_note-A_Guide-289] dug up by accident in the 1960s.[^cite_note-Frommer's_Greece_Forum_1-288] The forum complex also boasts two [Roman baths](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_baths),[^cite_note-A_Companion_1-290] one of which has been excavated while the other is buried underneath the city.[^cite_note-A_Companion_1-290] The forum also features a small theater,[^cite_note-Frommer's_Greece_Forum_1-288][^cite_note-A_Companion_1-290] which was also used for [gladiatorial games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gladiatorial_games).[^cite_note-A_Guide-289] Although the initial complex was not built in Roman times, it was largely refurbished in the second century.[^cite_note-A_Companion_1-290] It is believed that the forum and the theater continued to be used until at least the sixth century.[^cite_note-From_Early_Roman-291]

Another important archaeological site is the imperial palace complex which Roman emperor [Galerius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galerius), located at [Navarinou Square](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Navarinou_Square), commissioned when he made Thessaloniki the capital of his portion of the [Roman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Empire).[^cite_note-Roman_Empire-47][^cite_note-Roman_Empire_2-48] The large octagonal portion of the complex, most of which survives to this day, is believed to have been an imperial throne room.[^cite_note-A_Guide-289] Various [mosaics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mosaic) from the palatial complex have also survived.[^cite_note-The_Archaeology-292] Some historians believe that the complex must have been in use as an imperial residence until the 11th century.[^cite_note-From_Early_Roman-291]



A fourth-century Roman column with a female figure in relief, perhaps the personification of Thessalonica.&#x20;

Not far from the palace itself is the [Arch of Galerius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arch_of_Galerius),[^cite_note-The_Archaeology-292] known colloquially as the *Kamara*. The arch was built to commemorate the emperor's campaigns against the [Persians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persia).[^cite_note-A_Guide-289][^cite_note-The_Archaeology-292] The original structure featured three arches;[^cite_note-A_Guide-289] however, only two full arches and part of the third survive to this day. Many of the arches' marble parts survive as well,[^cite_note-A_Guide-289] although it is mostly the brick interior that can be seen today.

Other monuments of the city's past, such as *[Las Incantadas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Las_Incantadas)*, a [Caryatid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caryatid) portico from the ancient forum, have been removed or destroyed over the years. Las Incantadas in particular are on display at the [Louvre](17546.md).[^cite_note-Frommer's_Greece_Forum_1-288][^cite_note-Skai_Incantadas-293] Thanks to a private donation of €180,000, it was announced on 6 December 2011 that a replica of Las Incantadas would be commissioned and later put on display in Thessaloniki.[^cite_note-Skai_Incantadas-293]

The construction of the [Thessaloniki Metro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thessaloniki_Metro) inadvertently started the largest [archaeological dig](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Excavation_\(archaeology\)) not only of the city, but of [Northern Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Greece); the dig spans 20 km<sup>2</sup> (7.7 sq mi) and has unearthed 300,000 individual artefacts from as early as the [Roman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Empire) and as late as the [Great Thessaloniki Fire of 1917](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Thessaloniki_Fire_of_1917).[^cite_note-am_83_deth-294][^cite_note-295] Ancient Thessaloniki's [Decumanus Maximus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Decumanus_Maximus) was also found and 75 metres (246 ft) of the marble-paved and column-lined road were unearthed along with shops, other buildings, and plumbing, prompting one scholar to describe the discovery as "the Byzantine [Pompeii](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pompeii)".[^cite_note-Byzantine_Pompeii-296] Some of the artefacts will be put on display inside the metro stations, while [Venizelou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Venizelou_metro_station) will feature the world's first open [archaeological site](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Archaeological_site) located within a metro station.[^cite_note-Excavations-297][^cite_note-you_go-298]

### Festivals

Main article: [Festivals of Thessaloniki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Festivals_of_Thessaloniki)



Olympion Theatre, seat of the [International Film Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thessaloniki_International_Film_Festival)

Thessaloniki is home of a number of festivals and events.[^cite_note-299] The [Thessaloniki International Fair](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thessaloniki_International_Fair) is the most important event to be hosted in the city annually, by means of economic development. It was first established in 1926[^cite_note-DETH-300] and takes place every year at the 180,000 m<sup>2</sup> (1,900,000 ft<sup>2</sup>) *Thessaloniki International Exhibition Centre*. The event attracts major political attention and it is customary for the [Prime Minister of Greece](273647.md) to outline his administration's policies for the next year during the event. Over 250,000 visitors attended the exposition in 2010.[^cite_note-301] The new [Art Thessaloniki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Art_Thessaloniki?action=edit\&redlink=1), is starting first time 29.10. – 1 November 2015 as an international contemporary art fair. The [Thessaloniki International Film Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Thessaloniki_Film_Festival) is established as one of the most important film festivals in [Southern Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Europe),[^cite_note-302] with a number of notable filmmakers such as [Francis Ford Coppola](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francis_Ford_Coppola), [Faye Dunaway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Faye_Dunaway), [Catherine Deneuve](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catherine_Deneuve), [Irene Papas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irene_Papas) and [Fatih Akın](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fatih_Ak%C4%B1n) taking part, and was established in 1960.[^cite_note-303] The [Documentary Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thessaloniki_Documentary_Festival), founded in 1999, has focused on documentaries that explore global social and cultural developments, with many of the films presented being candidates for [FIPRESCI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FIPRESCI) and Audience Awards.[^cite_note-304]

The Dimitria festival, founded in 1966 and named after the city's [patron saint](68055.md) of [St. Demetrius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Demetrius), has focused on a wide range of events including music, theatre, dance, local happenings, and exhibitions.[^cite_note-305] The [DMC DJ Championship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DMC_World_DJ_Championships) has been hosted at the International Trade Fair of Thessaloniki, has become a worldwide event for aspiring DJs and turntablists. The International Festival of Photography has taken place every February to mid-April.[^cite_note-306] Exhibitions for the event are sited in museums, heritage landmarks, galleries, bookshops and cafés. Thessaloniki also holds an annual International Book Fair.[^cite_note-307]

Between 1962–1997 and 2005–2008, the city also hosted the [Thessaloniki Song Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thessaloniki_Song_Festival),[^cite_note-corner-308] Greece's most important music festival, at [Alexandreio Melathron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexandreio_Melathron_Nikos_Galis_Hall).[^cite_note-309]

In 2012, the city hosted its first [pride parade](58168.md), *Thessaloniki Pride*, which took place between 22 and 23 June.[^cite_note-thessalonikipride-310] It has been held every year ever since, however in 2013 [transgender](19904525.md) people participating in the parade became victims of [police brutality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Police_brutality). The issue was soon settled by the government.[^cite_note-311] The city's [Greek Orthodox Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Orthodox_Church) leadership has consistently rallied against the event, but mayor Boutaris sided with *Thessaloniki Pride*, with Thessaloniki also hosting [EuroPride](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/EuroPride) in 2024.[^cite_note-312] Since 1998, the city host Thessaloniki International G.L.A.D. Film Festival, the first LGBT film festival in Greece.

### Sports



[Kaftanzoglio National Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kaftanzoglio_Stadium)

The main stadium of the city is the [Kaftanzoglio National Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kaftanzoglio_Stadium) (also home ground of [Iraklis F.C.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraklis_F.C._\(Thessaloniki\))) constructed by donating funds of Thessalonian Jurist, Diplomat and Filanthropist Lysimachos Kaftantzoglou, while other main stadiums of the city include the football [Toumba Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Toumba_Stadium) and [Kleanthis Vikelidis Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kleanthis_Vikelidis_Stadium) home grounds of [PAOK FC](887670.md) and [Aris F.C.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aris_Thessaloniki_F.C.), respectively, all of whom are founding members of the [Greek league](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Super_League_Greece).

Being the largest "multi-sport" stadium in the city and in Greece until the construction of the [Olympic Stadium “ *Spìros Louis* ”](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olympic_Stadium_\(Athens\)) in Athens, [Kaftanzoglio Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kaftanzoglio_Stadium) regularly plays host to [athletics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Athletics_\(sport\)) events; such as the [European Athletics Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Athletics_Association) event "Olympic Meeting Thessaloniki" every year; it has hosted the National Greek Cup in 2009 and has been used for [athletics at the Mediterranean Games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Athletics_at_the_Mediterranean_Games) and for the [European Cup in athletics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Cup_\(athletics\)). In 2004, the stadium served as an official [Athens 2004](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2004_Summer_Olympics) venue,[^cite_note-313] while in 2009 the city and the stadium hosted the [2009 IAAF World Athletics Final](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2009_IAAF_World_Athletics_Final).

Thessaloniki's major indoor arenas include the state-owned [Alexandreio Melathron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexandreio_Melathron_Nikos_Galis_Hall), officially known as Nikos Galis Hall, commemorating [Nikos (Nick) Galis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nikos_Galis), the greatest Greek basketball player, and [P.A.O.K. Sports Arena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/P.A.O.K._Sports_Arena) in the urban area of Kalamarià, home of the omonymous basketball team and venue of numerous music concerts, events and festivals, as well the [YMCA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/YMCA) (Youth Men's Christian Association) indoor hall. Other sporting clubs in the city include [Apollon Kalamarias](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apollon_Kalamarias_F.C.) based in [Kalamaria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kalamaria), [Agrotikos Asteras F.C.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agrotikos_Asteras_F.C.) based in [Evosmos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evosmos) and YMCA. Thessaloniki has a rich sporting history with its teams winning the first ever panhellenic [football](10568.md) championship (Aris FC),[^cite_note-314] [basketball](3921.md) (Iraklis BC),[^cite_note-galanissportsdata.com-315] and [water polo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Water_polo) (AC Aris)[^cite_note-316] tournaments.

[PAOK FC](887670.md) has emerged as the strongest football club of the city, winning the Greek championship without a defeat ([2018–19 season](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018%E2%80%9319_Super_League_Greece)), as well as 2023–24 season.

The city played a major role in the development of basketball in Greece. The local YMCA was the first to introduce the sport to the country, while [Iraklis B.C.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/G.S._Iraklis_B.C.) won the first ever Greek championship.[^cite_note-galanissportsdata.com-315] From 1982 to 1993 [Aris B.C.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aris_B.C.) dominated the league, regularly finishing in first place. In that period Aris won a total of 9 championships, 7 cups and one European Cup Winners' Cup. The city also hosted the [2003 FIBA Under-19 World Championship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2003_FIBA_Under-19_World_Championship) in which Greece came third. In volleyball, [Iraklis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraklis_V.C._\(Thessaloniki\)) has emerged since 2000 as one of the most successful teams in Greece[^cite_note-317] and Europe – see [2005–06 CEV Champions League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2005%E2%80%9306_CEV_Champions_League).[^cite_note-318] In October 2007, Thessaloniki also played host to the first Southeastern European Games.[^cite_note-319]

The city is also the finish point of the annual [Alexander The Great Marathon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_The_Great_Marathon), which starts at [Pella](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pella), in recognition of its Ancient Macedonian heritage.[^cite_note-320] There are also aquatic and athletic complexes such as *Ethniko* and *Poseidonio*.

### Media

Thessaloniki is home to the [ERT3](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ERT3) TV-channel and [Radio Macedonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radio_Macedonia_\(ERT3\)?action=edit\&redlink=1), both services of public owned–[Hellenic Broadcasting Corporation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hellenic_Broadcasting_Corporation) (ERT) operating in the city and broadcasting all over Greece.[^cite_note-321] The municipality of Thessaloniki also owns and operates three radio stations, namely FM100, FM101 and FM100.6; and TV100, a television network which was also the first non-[state-owned](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State-owned) TV station in Greece and opened in 1988. Several private TV-networks also broadcast out from Thessaloniki, with [Makedonia TV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Makedonia_TV) being the most popular.

The city's main newspapers and some of the most circulated in Greece, include *[Makedonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Makedonia_\(newspaper\))*, which was also the first newspaper published in Thessaloniki in 1911 and *Aggelioforos*. A large number of radio stations also broadcast from Thessaloniki as the city is known for its music contributions.
