### Cuisine

See also: [Macedonian cuisine (Greek)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Macedonian_cuisine_\(Greek\))



[Frappé coffee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frapp%C3%A9_coffee)



[Bougatsa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bougatsa), typical Thessalonian treat

Because Thessaloniki remained under [Ottoman](22278.md) rule for about 100 years longer than southern Greece, it has retained a lot of its Eastern character, including its culinary tastes.[^cite_note-Frommer's_Food-326] Spices in particular play an important role in the cuisine of Thessaloniki,[^cite_note-Frommer's_Food-326] something which is not true to the same degree about Greece's southern regions.[^cite_note-Frommer's_Food-326] Thessaloniki's Ladadika borough is a particularly busy area in regards to Thessalonian cuisine, with most [tavernas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taverna) serving traditional [meze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meze) and other such culinary delights.[^cite_note-Frommer's_Food-326]

[Bougatsa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bougatsa), a breakfast [pastry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pastry), which can be either sweet with Patisserie créme or savory, is very popular throughout the city and has spread around other parts of Greece and the Balkans as well. Another popular snack is *[koulouri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Simit)*.

Notable sweets of the city are *Trigona Panoràmatos*, *Roxákia*, *[Kourkoubinia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kourkoubinia?action=edit\&redlink=1)&#x20;*&#x20;and *Armenonville*. A stereotypical Thessalonian [coffee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coffee) drink is [Frappé coffee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frapp%C3%A9_coffee). Frappé was invented in the [Thessaloniki International Fair](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thessaloniki_International_Fair) in 1957 and has since spread throughout Greece and Cyprus to become a hallmark of the Greek [coffee culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coffee_culture). Rather than Frappé, Thessalonians tend to enjoy drinking *Freddo Espresso*, a special Greek variation of Espresso coffee; specifically, after being extracted from the coffee machine, espresso is put into a shaker full of ice and eventually sugar (if preferred such) and afterwards it is frothed with an electric frother. Finally, it is served with ice and straw in a tall glass. Coffee is an inseparable part of Thessalonians, marking a quick break from work, a socialising opportunity among friends, family and couples as well as a chance to study, meet new people and relax.

*Kapani* or *Agora Vlalì* is the oldest central market in Thessaloniki, with shops selling fish, meat, vegetables, fruits, drinks, olives, sweets, nuts, spices[^cite_note-327][^cite_note-328][^cite_note-329] and [Modiano Market](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modiano_Market) is located nearby.[^cite_note-330]

### Tourism



Hotel Luxemvourgo on Komninon Street (1924, arch. [Eli Modiano](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eli_Modiano?action=edit\&redlink=1))



View of the [Makedonia Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Makedonia_Palace) on the promenade

A tourism boom took place in the 2010s, during the years of mayor [Boutaris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yiannis_Boutaris), especially from the neighboring countries, Austria, Israel and Turkey. In 2010, overnight stays of foreign tourists in the city were around 250,000. In 2018, overnight stays of foreign tourists were estimated to reach 3,000,000 people. Thessaloniki is known as "[the city that never sleeps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_City_That_Never_Sleeps_\(nickname\))" and a "party capital" due to its thriving [nightlife](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nightlife), young atmosphere and famous [24-hour culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/24/7).[^cite_note-lonelyplanet.com-331]

### Music

The city is viewed as a romantic one in Greece, and as such Thessaloniki is commonly featured in [Greek songs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_of_Greece).[^cite_note-332] There are a number of famous songs that go by the name 'Thessaloniki' ([rebetiko](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rebetiko), [laïko](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La%C3%AFko) etc.) or include the name in their title.[^cite_note-333]

During the 1930s and 1940s, the city became a centre of the [Rebetiko](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rebetiko) music, partly because of the [Metaxas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/4th_of_August_Regime) censorship, which was stricter in Athens. [Vassilis Tsitsanis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vassilis_Tsitsanis) wrote some of his best songs in Thessaloniki.

The city is the birthplace of significant composers in the Greek music scene, such as [Manolis Chiotis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manolis_Chiotis), [Stavros Kouyioumtzis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stavros_Kouyioumtzis) and [Dionysis Savvopoulos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dionysis_Savvopoulos). It is also notable for its rock music scene and its many rock groups; some became famous such as [Xylina Spathia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xylina_Spathia), [Trypes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trypes) or the [pop rock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pop_rock) group [Onirama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Onirama).

Between 1962–1997 and 2005–2008, the city also hosted the [Thessaloniki Song Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thessaloniki_Song_Festival). In the [Eurovision Song Contest 2013](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurovision_Song_Contest_2013) Greece was represented by [Koza Mostra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Koza_Mostra) and [Agathonas Iakovidis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agathonas_Iakovidis), both from Thessaloniki.

It should be highlighted that due to many Asia Minor and Pontian refugees from the genocide of 1923, Thessaloniki is the birthplace of many known traditional singers, such as [Chrysanthos Theodoridis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chrysanthos_Theodoridis) and lyra musician "Patriarch of Lyra" [Gogos Petridis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gogos_Petridis?action=edit\&redlink=1).

### In popular culture

In May 1936, a massive strike by tobacco workers led to general anarchy in the city and [Ioannis Metaxas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ioannis_Metaxas) (future dictator, then PM) ordered its repression. The events and the deaths of the protesters inspired poet [Yiannis Ritsos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yiannis_Ritsos) to write the *Epitafios*.

On 22 May 1963, [Grigoris Lambrakis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grigoris_Lambrakis) MD, physician-doctor, pacifist and MP, was assassinated by two far-right extremists driving a three-wheeled vehicle. The event led to the political crisis as it is presumed that State Gendarmerie, Army and Government members were indirectly coinvolved, even though never officially demonstrated. [Costa Gavras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Costa_Gavras) directed *[Z](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Z_\(1969_film\))* (1969) based on it, two years after the [military junta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_military_junta_of_1967%E2%80%9374) had seized power in Greece. For his notable juridical, incorruptible and unprejudiced work regarding Lambrakis’ case, First Instance Judge Christos Sartzetakis (Thessaloniki 1929–2022) was proposed and elected at the constitutionally highest political rank, becoming President of The Hellenic Republic.

Notable films set or shot in Thessaloniki, among others, include:

- *[Street of Shadows](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Street_of_Shadows_\(1937_film\))* (1937) by [Georg Wilhelm Pabst](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georg_Wilhelm_Pabst)

- *The Barefooted Battalion* (1954) by [Gregg Tallas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gregg_G._Tallas)

- *O Atsídas* (1961) by [Giannis Dalianidis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Giannis_Dalianidis)

- *Parenthesis* (1968) by [Takis Kanellopoulos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Takis_Kanellopoulos)

- [*Z*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Z_\(1969_film\)) (1969) by [Costa-Gavras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Costa-Gavras)

- *[Triumph of the Spirit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Triumph_of_the_Spirit)* (1989) by [Robert M. Young](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_M._Young_\(director\))

- *[Eternity and a Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eternity_and_a_Day)* (1998) by [Theo Angelopoulos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theo_Angelopoulos)

- *[Cloudy Sunday](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cloudy_Sunday_\(film\))* (2015) by [Manousos Manousakis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manousos_Manousakis)

- *[The Bricklayer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Bricklayer)* (2023) by [Renny Harlin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renny_Harlin)

## Education

See also: [Aristotle University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aristotle_University) and [University of Macedonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Macedonia)

Aerial view of the campus of the [Aristotle University of Thessaloniki](715160.md) (to the right), the largest university in Greece and the Balkans

Thessaloniki is a major centre of education for Greece. Three of the country's largest universities are located in central Thessaloniki: [Aristotle University of Thessaloniki](715160.md), the [University of Macedonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Macedonia) and the [International Hellenic University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Hellenic_University)–ex Intermediate Technical Educating Institution “Alexandros”. Aristotle University was founded in 1926 and is currently the largest university in [Greece](522222.md)[^cite_note-AUTH-13] by arithmetic enrolled students, which numbered more than 80,000 students of all degrees, classes and levels in 2010,[^cite_note-AUTH-13] and also a member of the [Utrecht Network](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Utrecht_Network). For the academic year *2009–2010*, [Aristotle University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aristotle_University) was ranked as one of the 150 best universities in the world for *arts and humanities* and among the 250 best universities in the world overall by the [Times QS World University Rankings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Times_Higher_Education-QS_World_University_Rankings),[^cite_note-QS-334] making it one of the top 2% of best universities worldwide.[^cite_note-335] Leiden ranks Aristotle University as one of the top 100 European universities, at number 97, and the best university in Greece.[^cite_note-336] Since 2010, Thessaloniki is also home to the Open University of Thessaloniki,[^cite_note-337] which is funded by Aristotle University, the University of Macedonia and the municipality of Thessaloniki.

Numerous public and private [vocational institutes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IEK) ([Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_language): *IEK*) provide professional non-accademica training to young students, while a large number of private colleges offer [American](3434750.md) and [UK](31717.md) academic curriculum and titles, via cooperation with foreign universities. In addition to [Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_people) students, the city hence attracts many foreign students either via the [Erasmus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Erasmus) programme for public universities, or for a complete degree in public universities or in the city's private colleges. As of 2006 the city's total student population was estimated around 200,000.[^cite_note-338]

## Transport

### Tram



The old tram lines on Agiou Mina Street

[Tram](30733.md) was the main, oldest and most popular public urban mean of Thessalonians in the past. It was in operation from 1893 to 1957, when it was disestablished by the government of [Konstantinos Karamanlis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Konstantinos_Karamanlis). The Belgian *Compagnie de Tramways et d' Éclairage Électrique de Salonique* operated it from 1912 until 1940, when the company was purchased by the Hellenic State. The operating base and tram station was in the district of Dépôt.

Before the [economic crisis of 2009](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_government-debt_crisis), there were various proposals for new tram lines.[^cite_note-339]

### Bus&#x20;

Main article: [Thessaloniki Urban Transport Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thessaloniki_Urban_Transport_Organization)



An [OASTH](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/OASTH) bus

[Thessaloniki Urban Transport Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thessaloniki_Urban_Transport_Organization) (*OASTH*) operates public transport buses in Thessaloniki. It was founded in 1957 and operates a fleet of 604 vehicles on 75 routes throughout the [Thessaloniki metropolitan area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thessaloniki_metropolitan_area).[^cite_note-340] International and regional bus links are provided by [KTEL](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/KTEL_\(Greece\)) at its [“Macedonia” Extraurban Bus Terminal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thessaloniki_Bus_Station), located to the west of the city centre.[^cite_note-341]

[Agias Sofias metro station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agias_Sofias_metro_station)

The creation of a metro system for Thessaloniki goes back as far as 1918, when [Thomas Hayton Mawson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Hayton_Mawson) and [Ernest Hébrard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ernest_H%C3%A9brard) proposed the creation of a Thessaloniki Metropolitan Railway.[^cite_note-342] In 1968, a circular metro line was proposed, and in 1987 the first serious proposal was presented and construction briefly started in 1988, before stalling and finally being abandoned due to lack of funding.[^cite_note-Karfitsa-343] Both the 1918 and 1988 proposals ran almost the identical route to the current [Line 1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Line_1_\(Thessaloniki_Metro\)).

Construction of Thessaloniki metro began in 2006 and is classified as a [megaproject](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Megaproject): it has a budget of €1.57 billion (\$1.77 billion).[^cite_note-Funding-344] [Line 1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Line_1_\(Thessaloniki_Metro\)) began operation on 30 November 2024, while [Line 2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Line_2_\(Thessaloniki_Metro\)) entered service on 27 August 2026. Line 1 is 9.5 kilometres (5.9 mi) long and includes 13 stations, while Line 2 is 4.8 kilometres (3.0 mi) long and includes a further five stations, while also crossing 11 of the 13 Line 1 stations.[^cite_note-History_Metro-345][^cite_note-synenteuksi_mylopoulou-346] [Important archaeological discoveries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thessaloniki_Metro#Archaeology) have been made during construction, and some of the system's stations house archaeological exhibitions.[^cite_note-AF-347] One station in particular, that of [Venizelou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Venizelou_metro_station), houses the only archaeological site within a metro station in the world.[^cite_note-Skai_Metro-348]

[Line 2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Line_2_\(Thessaloniki_Metro\)) is to be expanded further to the northwestern suburbs of the city, towards [Evosmos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evosmos) and [Stavroupoli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stavroupoli), and to the east, towards [the Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thessaloniki_Airport_%22Makedonia%22).[^cite_note-Circular-349] The northwestern extension is a priority, as the airport is served by bus to the terminus of Line 1.

It is now expected that 320,000 people per day and 116 million people per year will use the Thessaloniki metro.[^cite_note-Move-350]

### Commuter/suburban rail (Proastiakos)

Main article: [Proastiakos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proastiakos)



Suburban Railway services

Commuter intraregional rail services have recently been established between Thessaloniki and the city of [Larissa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Larissa) (the service is known in [Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_language) as the "[Proastiakos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proastiakos)", meaning "Suburban Railway"). The service is operated using [Siemens Desiro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siemens_Desiro) EMU trains on a modernised electrified [double track](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Double_track) and stops at 11 restructured rail stations, covering the journey in 1 hour and 33 minutes.[^cite_note-351] Furthermore, an additional line has also been established, with the use of regional trains, connecting Thessaloniki to [Edessa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edessa,_Greece) and [Florina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Florina).

### Thessaloniki Airport "Makedonia"



[Thessaloniki International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thessaloniki_International_Airport)

International and domestic [air traffic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Air_traffic) to and from the city of Thessaloniki is served by the ["Makedonia" International Airport of Thessaloniki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thessaloniki_Airport_%22Makedonia%22). The short length of the airport's two runways means that it does not, yet, support intercontinental flights; however, a major extension of one of its runways into the [Thermaic Gulf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thermaic_Gulf) is currently under construction.[^cite_note-352] Following the completion of the runway works, the airport will be able to serve intercontinental flights and cater for larger aircraft in the future. After long delays, the new runway of the airport was completed in spring 2019. Construction of a second new terminal began in September 2018.[^cite_note-Naftemporiki-353] New T2 has been finished in February 2021, three months ahead of schedule.[^cite_note-354]

### Railways

The [Thessaloniki railway station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thessaloniki_railway_station) features a central hall with a shopping centre within a 1960s era building. The station has staffed ticketing services, a large climate-controlled waiting room, café bar, fast-food restaurants, kiosk and luggage lockers. On the platforms, cover seating is available and the station is alongside a bus terminal. The station is connected by bus and the Metro with much of the urban area of Thessaloniki, including other major transport centres such as the Macedonia InterCity Bus Terminal ([KTEL](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/KTEL_\(Greece\))), the port and [Macedonia International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Macedonia_International_Airport).

&#x20;Regional train services within Greece (operated by [TrainOSE](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TrainOSE), the [Hellenic Railways Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hellenic_Railways_Organization)'s train operating company), link the city with other main urban parts of the country's territory, indicatively Athens, Larissa, Katerìni, Edessa, Véria, Florina, Serres, Alexandroupoli, Orestiada, Drama, Xànthi, Meteora and more. Once there were abundant international links serving different European and Balkan cities and also remarkable trains such as Orient Express, Acropolis Express, Hellas Express, Eurocity ecc. namely; Sofia, Belgrade, Skopje, Vienna, Budapest, Bucharest, Istanbul and most importantly diverse German cities.



[New railway station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_railway_station_\(Thessaloniki\))

Due to the Greek economic crisis, all international train links from the city were suspended in February 2011.[^cite_note-355] Until then, the city was a major railway hub for the Balkans and Eastern Europe. Daily through trains to Sofia (via Serres) and Belgrade (via Gevgelija) were restarted in May 2014 but stopped again due to COVID-19 restrictions. Nevertheless, Thessaloniki remains a key passenger and merchant railway hub with the biggest [marshalling yard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marshalling_yard) in the country and still one of the biggest in Balkans.

### Port

Main article: [Port of Thessaloniki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Thessaloniki)

The [Port of Thessaloniki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Thessaloniki) connects the city with seasonal ferries to the [Sporades](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sporades), other north Aegean islands and Turkish Minor Asia's ports like Izmir/Smyrni, with its [passenger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Passenger) terminal being one of the largest in the [Aegean Sea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aegean_Sea) basin; having handled around 162,731 [passengers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Passengers) in 2007.[^cite_note-356] Meanwhile, ongoing actions have been going on for more connections and the port is recently being upgraded, as Thessaloniki is also slowly turning into a major tourist port for cruising in the eastern Mediterranean. It also consists the second largest port of Greece by merchant traffic volume being only behind Piraeus Port, the largest domestic port.

### Motorways

Further information: [Highways in Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Highways_in_Greece)



Road map of Thessaloniki and its suburbs from [OpenStreetMap](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/OpenStreetMap)



Part of the [ring road](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ring_road) (Peripheriaki Odos)

Thessaloniki lies on the crossroads of the [A1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A1_motorway_\(Greece\))/[E75](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_route_E75), [A2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A2_motorway_\(Greece\))/[E90](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_route_E90) and [A25](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A25_motorway_\(Greece\)) motorways; which connect the city with other parts of the country, as well as the neighbouring countries of [North Macedonia](23564616.md), [Bulgaria](3415.md) and [Turkey](11125639.md).

The city itself is bypassed by the C-shaped [Thessaloniki Inner Ring Road](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thessaloniki_Inner_Ring_Road) (*Esoteriki Peripheriaki Odos*, [Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_language): Εσωτερική Περιφεριακή Οδός), which all of the above motorways connect onto it. The western end of the route begins at the junction with the [A1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A1_motorway_\(Greece\))/[A2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A2_motorway_\(Greece\)) motorways in *Lachanagora* District. Clockwise it heads northeast around the city, passing through the northwestern suburbs, the forest of *Seich Sou* and through to the southeast suburb/borough of [Kalamaria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kalamaria). The ring road ends at a large junction with the [A24](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A24_motorway_\(Greece\)) motorway, which then continues south to [Chalkidiki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chalkidiki), passing through [Thessaloniki's](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thessaloniki_metropolitan_area) outer southeast suburbs.

The speed limit on this motorway is 90 kilometres per hour (56 mph); it currently has three traffic lanes for each direction and forms the city's most vital road link; handling more than 120,000 vehicles daily,[^cite_note-357] instead of the 30,000 vehicles that it was originally designed to handle in 1975.[^cite_note-358] An outer [ring road](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ring_road) known as **Eksoteriki Peripheriaki Odos** ([Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_language): Εξωτερική Περιφεριακή Οδός, *outer ring road*) carries all traffic that completely bypasses the city. It is part of [A2 motorway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A2_motorway_\(Greece\)).[^cite_note-359]

#### Future plans



Taxi in Thessaloniki

Despite the large effort that was made in 2004 to improve the motorway features of the Thessaloniki [ring road](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ring_road), the motorway is still insufficient to handle Thessaloniki's increasing traffic and metropolitan population. To tackle this problem, the government has introduced large scale redevelopment plans throughout 2011[^cite_note-2011_Ring_Road_works-360] with tenders expected to be announced within early 2012;[^cite_note-2011_Ring_Road_works-360] that include the total restructuring of the A16 in the western side of the city, with new junctions and new [emergency lanes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hard_shoulder) throughout the whole length of the motorway.[^cite_note-2011_Ring_Road_works-360] In the eastern side an even larger scale project has been announced, for the construction of a new elevated motorway section above the existing, which would allow faster travel for drivers heading to the [airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thessaloniki_International_Airport,_%22Macedonia%22) and [Chalkidiki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chalkidiki) that do not wish to exit into the city, decongesting the existing motorway for city commuters.[^cite_note-2011_Ring_Road_redevelopment_plans-361] The plans also include adding one more lane in each direction on the existing A16 ring road and on the [A24](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A24_motorway_\(Greece\)) passing through [Thessaloniki's](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thessaloniki_metropolitan_area) southeast suburbs, from its junction with the A16 in [Kalamaria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kalamaria), up to the airport exit ([ΕΟ67](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_National_Road_67)); which will make it an 8 lane highway.[^cite_note-2011_Ring_Road_works-360]

Additional long-term plans include the extension of the planned outer [ring road](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ring_road) known as **Eksoteriki Peripheriaki Odos** ([Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_language): Εξωτερική Περιφεριακή Οδός, *outer ring road*) to circle around the entirety of the [Thessaloniki metropolitan area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thessaloniki_metropolitan_area), crossing over the Thermaic Gulf from the east, to join with the [A1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A1_motorway_\(Greece\))/[E75](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_route_E75) motorway. Preliminary plans have been announced which include a 4.5 km (3 mi) bridge over the gulf, as part of the southern bypass of the city; to cater for the large number of travellers from Macedonia and the rest of Greece heading to the [airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thessaloniki_International_Airport,_%22Macedonia%22), and to the increasingly popular tourist region of [Chalkidiki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chalkidiki).[^cite_note-362]

Thessaloniki is also planned to be the southern terminus of the trans-European [Via Carpathia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Via_Carpathia) motorway.

[^cite_note-urban_metro-4]: &#x20;Thessaloniki is an urban area defined in 1985 through Law 1561/1985. Since the [Kallikratis reform](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kallikratis_Plan) it has been made up of the municipalities of Thessaloniki (325,182), [Kalamaria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kalamaria) (91,518), [Neapoli–Sykies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neapoli-Sykies) (84,741), [Pavlos Melas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pavlos_Melas_\(municipality\)) (99,245), [Kordelio–Evosmos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kordelio-Evosmos) (101,753), [Ampelokipoi–Menemeni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ampelokipoi-Menemeni) (52,127), and the municipal units of [Pylaia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pylaia) and [Panorama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Panorama,_Thessaloniki) (34,625 and 17,444; part of the municipality of [Pylaia–Chortiatis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pylaia-Chortiatis)). The [Thessaloniki metropolitan area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thessaloniki_metropolitan_area) was defined by the same law and is made up of the urban area plus the municipalities of [Delta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delta,_Thessaloniki) (45,839), [Oraiokastro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oraiokastro) (38,317), [Thermaikos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thermaikos) (50,264), [Thermi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thermi) (53,201), and the municipal unit of [Chortiatis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chortiatis) (18,041; part of the municipality of [Pylaia–Chortiatis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pylaia-Chortiatis)), for a total of 1,030,338.
